CASE B1:  A zirconia bridge 
 
This Cercon CAM (computer assisted machining) prepared zirconia bridge fractured in 
vivo 2003.  No documentation was furnished and only one piece was retrieved.  Figure 1 shows 
that the fracture occurred at the connector to an end molar unit.  The connector had a good cross 
section size.    Figure 1b appeared in the Guide to Fractography as illustration (Figure 4.7a) of 
how a large sintering crack could be present in a ceramic, but not necessarily act as a fracture 
origin.  Figure 2 shows the fracture surface and radiating hackle lines led back to the origin site. 
The origin was on the underside of the connector was grinding cracking that generated 
“machining crack hackle” once fracture occurred.  These are described in detail in Refs. 
11,12,13.  They are much easier to see with a low power optical microscope (Figure 2a) than 
with the scanning electron microscope (SEM) (Figure 2b). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 3.  Case B2.   A laboratory fractured zirconia bridge.  (a) shows the gold-coated incisor 
end unit.  The connector size is too small.  The small arrow in (a) shows a compression curl.  The 
large arrow shows the origin site. (b) shows the fracture surface and (c) shows the origin which is 
damage in the veneer (circled) that propagated a crack into the ceramic core.

CASE B2:  A training course zirconia bridge 
 
This Cercon CAD zirconia bridge fractured in the lab in 2003 during fabrication training.  
Only an incisor end unit (Fig. 3) was available.  The connector size (  2 mm) was too small.  The 
low magnification image (Figure 3a) shows a compression curl which is indicative of a bending 
fracture.  The origin is located opposite to it.  This bridge broke primarily from a sideways force 
probably from mishandling or possibly during the grinding.  SEM images (Figures 3b,c) show 
the fracture surface is very smooth and almost featureless, indicative of a low energy, low 
strength fracture.  The origin was microcracking and bubbles in the veneer.  They combined to 
make a crack that propagated into the core.  Additional SEM images (not shown) show the core 
crack progressed in stages with multiple concentric semielliptical arrest lines.  
 
CASE B3:  A training course four-unit posterior zirconia bridge  
 
 
This also was a training course restoration.   Figure 4a shows that this bridge was 
designed to be supported by three abutments.  Fracture occurred at one connector to the pontic.   
This connector was too small.  The location of the compression curl indicated that this bridge 
also broke from sideways flexure.  It also was a low energy, low strength fracture.  Optical 
photos in Figure 5 confirmed that the origin comprised irregularities in the veneer as well as 
“zipper machining cracks”  in the core ceramic from machining.    
 
Figure 4.  Case B3:  A four-unit posterior zirconia bridge in (a).   The piece on the left is gold 
coated. (b) shows the fracture surfaces with the piece’s occlusal surfaces mounted back-to-back.  
Compression curls are marked with small arrows.  The origin is marked by the large arrows.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 5.  Case B3.  Optical views of the fracture surface.  (a) and (b) are gold coated. The origin 
is a series of small grinding cracks in the core that were covered by the veneer.  Gold coating and 
vicinal shadow illumination make the “zipper machining crack” hackle lines easy to see with 
optical examination.  They were not evident with high magnification SEM examination. (c) is a 
simple optical image of the uncoated fracture surface taken by Dr. J. Quinn in 2003 that also 
shows the grinding crack hackle and veneer irregularities.

CASE B4:   A three-unit alumina bridge 
 
This was a three-unit anterior maxillary bridge, shown in Figure 6, which fractured at the 
connector to the right central incisor in mid-2009 after approximately four years in the patient’s 
mouth.  This case was presented in some detail in Ref. 9 and this case included here for 
comparison to the other bridge cases. The bridge was cemented to abutments on the end two 
units #8 and #10.  The clinician who donated the broken FPD detected a crack in the incisor (#8) 
during a routine checkup and monitored its progress with time.  When fracture occurred, he was 
able to retrieve units #8 and #9 on either side of the fracture plane.  The clinician believed that 
the restoration was a zirconia, but our analysis showed that it was actually glass-infused alumina.   
 
 
 
Figure 6.   Case B4.  The three-unit anterior maxillary bridge which broke through the side of the 
incisor unit #8 as shown in (a).   Crack propagation occurred as shown by the arrows in (b).  The 
triangular broken fragment was reattached for this photo. 
 
Figure 6b shows a close-up with arrows marking the direction of crack propagation (dcp) 
away from the origin site A, which was on the margin.  Figure 7 shows the fracture surface.  The 
crack went down the side wall of #8 to the incisal edge.   It then went around the restoration and 
caused a large triangular piece to break off.  The origin region is shown in Figures 7a and 8.  
There were no severe material irregularities or gross flaws at the margin.  The lack of a fracture 
mirror indicated the fracture stress was low.  The margin was well-prepared and had a smooth 
rounded edge.  Nevertheless, there is a small crack at the bottom, and combined with hoop 
stresses acting on the rim of the unit (presumably created by occlusal biting forces), the crack 
propagated up the thin wall of the tooth and split it.  This is a failure mode that has been 
observed before in a number of single crowns, especially if they are very thin or if the margin is 
damaged during manufacture or installation.     As a consequence, it is strongly advised to not 
use “knife-edge” or “feather” margins, which some clinicians like to use for aesthetic reasons.   
Such restoration margins are simply too vulnerable even if a strong ceramic is used.   
 
Figure 7b shows an SEM image of a polished portion of a fragment from core ceramic 
portion of the specimen.  It is a two-phase material with residual porosity.  The primary 
constituents were aluminum, calcium, lanthanum and silicon.  This is not a zirconia restoration, 
but most likely that of a glass-infused alumina and is likely Vita Inceram alumina.

CASE B5:  Three (or more) unit zirconia bridge 
 
This was a three (or more) unit anterior bridge, shown in Figure 9, which fractured in 
European unit #25 near to but not at the connector in 2011 after 4 years in vivo.  This case has 
not been documented previously.  Only three pieces were obtained from one end of the 
restoration and the documentation was incomplete. Two of the three fragments were separated 
during extraction.  The clinical fracture face was on the left on unit #27 as seen in the figure. 
Units #27 and #26 were fitted to abutments, but #25 was cantilevered. The restoration was 
prepared during a training course and was designed by an experienced elder dental technician, 
but fabricated by an unknown attendee in the course. The bridge framework was made with a 
Sirona copy milling machine.   The fracture surface (Figs. 10 and 11) and the underside of the 
proximal connector (Fig. 9b) had very unusual markings.  The veneer was badly crazed on the 
connector underside, indicating an improper composition or application, or improper furnace 
operation.

Energy dispersive spectroscopy revealed that (on a side of the bridge well away from the 
fracture location) the veneer contained elements typical of a feldspathic veneer: Si, K, Ca plus 
traces of Ti, Cr, Fe, Cu, Zn in addition to some signals of Zr, Y and Al from the core, but no La.  
The veneer glass composition on the connector underside where the crazing occurred was 
identified as Si, K, Ca, Cu or Zn, and some La as well as the Zr and Al from the core.  The 
abnormal material on the rough part of the fracture surface was composed of strong Zr, Y, Al, Si, 
La, K, Ca, Zn, and possible Ti.   The Zr, Y, Al, Si, and La are characteristic of Inceram AZ 
material, but the material in the rough zone appeared very glassy and was transparent, unlike the 
core material exposed at the top of the fracture surface.   
 
No distinct fracture origin was detected, but on the basis of the extremely unusual 
fracture surface morphology and glassy structure of the framework material in the fracture site, it 
is believed that this bridge was cracked during a step in the fabrication process.  Glassy material 
from the core and veneer “healed” the crack and the restoration survived in vivo for 4 years.  
One wonders whether the veneer crazing on the underside of the connector should have warned 
the technician or the clinician that something was amiss.  The connector also was too small: 
measuring only 2 mm to 2.5 mm in height. 
   
CASE B6:  Three-unit e.max Press lithium disilicate bridge 
 
This three unit bridge broke in 2011 after seven weeks in vivo (Figure 12).  When the 
patient was seen by the dentist, both connectors were broken, but the pontic section was still in 
place from mechanical interlocking.  The connector shown on the right (Fig. 12c) fractured first, 
and continued occlusal loading and usage caused the second connector (Fig. 12a) to break from 
bending.  Only the middle unit, the pontic was available for examination, but fortunately it had  
 
 
Figure 12.  Case B6. Middle molar unit of a three-unit bridge.  The wing photos (a) and (c) show 
the fractured connectors on each side. (d) and (e) are close-ups of both broken connectors after 
gold coating.  The right connector broke first and has a flatter and straighter fracture plane.

both fracture planes.  The connector cross section sizes were probably too small for this glass 
ceramic.  The secondary fracture started with an origin at the top.  There was a lot of twisting 
and bending of this fracture surface, and this location suggested that this fracture was the second 
fracture.  The primary origin was on the right side from the gingival-lingual area.  Close-up 
examination (Figure 13) revealed that the origin was a single small contact damage site on the 
veneer outer surface.  Cracking propagated in short steps through the veneer, and then into the 
core, also in a few steps until final catastrophic fracture occurred.  Close-up photos of the contact 
site (not shown here due to space limitations) reveal classical tertiary Wallner lines and localized 
hackle radiation right at the contact site.  Since it is down on the inside of the mouth, the patient 
would have had difficulty inflicting damage at this site. It is more likely that a dental probe or 
tool in the dental lab created the initial damage.  
This is an excellent example of a serious 
issue in ceramics:  one small spot or vulnerable in a surface coating can act as a weak link.  The 
core ceramic itself can be nearly perfect, with no flaws, but once the crack initiates in the 
coating, it can easily propagate into the bulk.   
 
 
Figure 13.  Case B6.   SEM close ups of the origin.  Radiating hackle lines converge on the 
origin region.  The small arrows in (a) mark a subtle curved crack arrest line in the core ceramic, 
one of many such concentric arcs.  Close-up examination of the origin in (b) reveals tiny wake 
hackle lines radiating behind the pores, and multiple arrest lines (marked by small arrows).  
Fracture initiated at the single contact damage site in the veneer outer surface, and propagated in 
steps through the veneer and then on into the core ceramic.   
 
 
CASE B7:  A five-unit zirconia telescoping denture 
 
Three segments were used to replace the entire upper dental arch including substantial 
missing hard and soft tissues as shown in the left of Figure 14.  This case has been presented in 
detail previously.9  Six implant posts supported the segments.  The segment that fractured after 
installation was the five-unit telescoping denture made of a single large piece of veneered 
zirconia as shown in Figure 14a.  It ran from the upper left lateral incisor to the upper left first 
molar and was attached to two screw implants that anchored the part at the incisor and second 
premolar teeth.  The cantilevered end unit broke off the restoration in 2010 only 2 days after 
cementation. The patient exerted normal biting forces. This fracture was very surprising since the 
last molar was connected to the rest of the restoration by a very large 10 mm x 12 mm cross 
section. It is hard to imagine that a patient could apply sufficient biting forces to snap off such a

The zirconia was CAD/CAM machined from presintered zirconia blocks and then

that popped in from origin site A during processing.  The crack ran halfway through the piece 
and severely weakened it.  The second fracture occurred when the end molar finally broke off in 
the patient’s mouth.  Close-up SEM examination of the origin shown in Figure 15b revealed 
irregularities in the core ceramic surface plus some grinding adjustment damage. 
 
Very smooth, almost featureless, wavy fracture planes are commonly generated by 
thermal stress fractures.   These can occur from: sudden temperature changes, thermal gradients 
set up by non-uniform heating or cooling, or internal residual stresses set up during firing.   It 
cannot be ascertained with certainty whether the first crack formed during the cool down or after 
removal from the furnace.  It is very likely that the veneer covered a cracked foundation piece or 
the crack may have popped in during the firing of the veneer. 
 
CASE: C1 Incisor zirconia crown    
 
This case has nothing in common with the previous seven, with the exception that it too 
was a very unusual fracture.  It was provided by the clinician from Vienna, Austria who dutifully 
reported the material system, the dental laboratory, the duration prior to fracture and the fact that 
it broke during normal bite functioning.   Figure 16 shows the broken piece, which as subsequent 
analysis showed, was all porcelain veneer!  It appeared that it completely detached from the core 
zirconia ceramic.  Fractographic images with optical (Figure 17) and scanning electron 
microscopy (not shown) revealed that fracture started on the inside of the crown, about 0.5 mm 
below the outer crown surface.  The fracture was entirely within the two layer veneer, and 
propagated entirely around the tooth without penetrating into the zirconia at all. The zirconia part 
of the crown remained in the mouth attached to the abutment, but had to be extracted and 
replaced.    The veneer porcelain was primarily composed the usual elements of a feldspathic 
porcelain and included Si, Al, Ca, K, Fe but also a surprising amount of Cu.  The origin crack, 
and especially its location, is very unusual and suggestive of a thermal crack possibly created 
during cool down.  Figure 17c shows an extraordinary array of concentric semicircular cracks 
radiating outward from the origin.  These are arrest lines and probably correlate with fatigue 
stepwise growth of the crack. This fracture sequence is similar to another veneered zirconia 
crown analyzed by Lohbauer in 2011.15 In that case, the crack popped into the veneer from 
occlusal surface contact damage, and then curved around the porcelain and the interface avoiding 
the zirconia core.  There is steadily growing literature (e.g., Tholey et al., Ref. 16) showing that 
cool down rates through the glass transition temperature and thermal gradients in furnaces and 
restorations create strong residual tensile stresses in zirconia veneers.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 16.  Case C1.  Detached porcelain veneer on the incisor crown that broke in-vivo at 14 
months.  The side extension of the crown is termed a “handle” for orientation convenience.

Figure 17.  Case C1.  Images of the interior surface.  The fracture origin is an internal crack 
(large arrows) that started entirely in the outer veneer and then propagated into both porcelain 
layers in steps creating concentric arrest lines.  
 
DISCUSSION 
 
As noted in the introduction, it is important to identify the causes of fracture so that 
manufacturers, clinicians, and laboratory technicians can minimize the incidence of breakage.  
Identification of the mechanisms of fracture can help researchers can develop relevant testing 
procedures.  Table 1 shows that there were multiple causes of failure including faulty design of 
the restoration, faulty preparation, or damage from clinician handling or adjustments.   There 
were no fracture mirrors, so all the fractures occurred at relatively low stress levels.    
 
These examples reinforce some lessons learned in earlier analyses.  Design issues such as 
thin margins (Case B4) or connectors that are too small (Cases B2,B3,B5) are known 
contributors to fracture.  Grinding cracks from CAD machining (Cases B1-B3) can create 
strength limiting flaws that are often easier to see optically than in the SEM.  Other large 
(thermal) cracks may be created in the core material in the laboratory and may be sealed over by 
the veneer (Cases B5,B7) but severely compromise the integrity of the restoration.  Chipping and 
delamination of veneers from zirconia crowns (Case C1) can be a problem.  There also is a new 
growing literature of origin cracks forming in veneers, especially at concentrations of bubbles or 
from contact damage, and then propagating into the core.  The veneers are supposed to be in 
residual compression, but if they are irregular with bubble clusters (Case B6),8 too thin, or 
damaged by the lab or clinician (also Case B6), they can act as fracture initiation sites.  Careful 
application of veneers is important.   
 
 We are encouraged that fractographic analysis is increasingly being used to study 
clinical fractures, and that clinically relevant laboratory testing procedures are being developed 
as a result.17,18  These are far more valuable19 than “crunch the crown” type tests that were 
fashionable in the 1990s and 2000s.    
 
CONCLUSIONS 
 
Seven bridges and one crown fractures were analyzed.  Several fracture modes and 
origin types were identified.  These cases illustrate that all-ceramic restorations are susceptible to 
fracture from a variety of causes.  Proper design and fabrication procedures must be established 
and followed to eliminate in vivo fractures.

53
IN VIVO EVALUATION OF SCAFFOLDS WITH A GRID-LIKE MICROSTRUCTURE 
COMPOSED OF A MIXTURE OF SILICATE (13-93) AND BORATE (13-93B3) BIOACTIVE 
GLASSES 
 
Yifei Gu1, Wenhai Huang1, Mohamed N. Rahaman2 
1School of Materials Science and Engineering, Tongji University, Shanghai 200092, China 
2Department of Materials Science and Engineering and Center for Biomedical Science and 
Engineering, Missouri University of Science and Technology, Rolla, MO 65409-0340, USA 
 
ABSTRACT 
A borate bioactive glass (designated 13-93B3) converts faster to hydroxyapatite (HA) than 
silicate 13-93 bioactive glass but recent studies have shown conflicting results for its capacity to 
repair rat calvarial defects. In this study, scaffolds composed of 13-93 and 13-93B3 glass alone 
and composite scaffolds composed of a mixture of 13-93 and 13-93B3 glasses were created with 
the same grid-like microstructure (porosity = 47%; filament diameter = 330 μm; pore width = 
300 μm) using a robocasting technique. When implanted in rat calvarial defects in vivo, the 
scaffolds showed a decreasing capacity to regenerate bone with increasing volume fraction of 
13-93B3 glass in the scaffolds. The percent new bone in the defects implanted with the 13-93 
scaffolds (23 ± 4 % at 6 weeks and 28 ± 8% at 12 weeks) was significantly higher than in the 
defects implanted with the 13-93B3 scaffolds (6 ± 4 % at 6 weeks and 9 ± 7 % at 12 weeks). 
While the 13-93 glass was only partially converted to HA after 12 weeks in vivo, the 13-93B3 
glass was almost fully converted within 6 weeks. Composite scaffolds composed of an optimized 
mixture of silicate 13-93 and borate 13-93B3 bioactive glasses may provide advantages for bone 
and tissue healing over scaffolds composed of 13-93 or 13-93B3 glass alone.   
 
INTRODUCTION 
Large bone defects resulting from civilian and war trauma, malignancy, and congenital 
diseases are commonly encountered in orthopaedic practice [1, 2]. Autologous bone grafts, the 
gold standard for treatment, heal reliably but they are limited by availability and donor site 
morbidity [3]. Bone allografts are alternatives but they are expensive, suffer from uncertain 
healing to bone, and carry the risk of disease transmission and adverse host immune reaction [4]. 
Because of these disadvantages, the need for synthetic bone grafts continues to grow. 
Bioactive glasses have attractive properties as a scaffold material for bone repair because of 
their ability to convert to hydroxyapatite (HA), bond to bone in vivo, and enhance osteogenesis 
and revascularization [5ࡁ7]. The silicate glass designated 45S5 is the most widely studied 
bioactive glass and it has been widely available for commercial use since the 1980s [7]. Another 
silicate bioactive glass, designated 13-93, is also used clinically [6]. The composition of 13-93 
glass is based on 45S5 but the glass contains additional oxides that allow it to be more readily 
fabricated into anatomically relevant shapes [8]. Porous scaffolds of 13-93 glass with a grid-like 
microstructure have been created by a robotic deposition (robocasting) method with compressive 
strengths comparable to human cortical bone (100ࡁ150 MPa) [9ࡁ11], high mechanical reliability 
(high Weibull modulus) and excellent fatigue resistance [11].  
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
54
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
In Vivo Evaluation of Scaffolds with a Grid-Like Microstructure
A borate bioactive glass designated 13-93B3, obtained by replacing all of the SiO2 in silicate 
13-93 glass with B2O3, has been the subject of several recent studies [6]. Borate 13-93B3 glass 
has been shown to convert more rapidly to HA in an aqueous phosphate solution when compared 
to silicate bioactive glasses such as 45S5 and 13-93 [12ࡁ14]. Borate 13-93B3 glass can also be 
formed into porous 3-D structures [6]. Scaffolds of 13-93B3 glass with a “trabecular” 
microstructure similar to dry human trabecular bone have shown the capacity to support soft 
tissue infiltration when implanted in rat subcutaneous sites [15]. Scaffolds of 13-93B3 glass with 
a “fibrous” microstructure, composed of thermally-bonded short glass fibers, were found to have 
a greater capacity to regenerate bone in rat calvarial defects when compared to 13-93 scaffolds 
with a similar microstructure [16]. However, in another study, fibrous scaffolds showed a 
decreasing capacity to regenerate bone in rat calvarial defects with increasing volume fraction of 
13-93B3 glass in the scaffolds [17]; in particular, scaffolds composed of 13-93B3 glass alone 
showed a significantly lower capacity to regenerate bone than 13-93 scaffolds.   
In view of the conflicting results for the capacity of 13-93B3 scaffolds to regenerate bone 
[16, 17], this study was undertaken to create and evaluate scaffolds composed of 13-93 glass and 
varying volume fractions of 13-93B3 glass (0ࡁ100%). The scaffolds were fabricated by a robotic 
deposition (robocasting) technique to create the same grid-like microstructure. The capacity of 
the scaffolds to regenerate bone in rat calvarial defects was studied as a function of the volume 
fraction of 13-93B3 glass in the scaffolds.     
  
MATERIALS AND METHODS 
 
Fabrication of bioactive glass scaffolds 
The silicate 13-93 and borate 13-93B3 bioactive glasses used in this study were prepared by 
conventional melting and casting; they were provided by Mo-Sci Corp., Rolla, MO. The 
compositions of the glasses are given in Table I. Four groups of scaffolds with the same grid-like 
microstructure were prepared using a robocasting technique. Two groups were composed of 
13-93 and 13-93B3 glass alone. The other two groups were composed of an inner cylindrical 
region of 13-93 glass and an outer axially concentric layer of 13-93B3 glass (see Fig. 1). Two 
different thicknesses were used to vary the volume fraction of 13-93B3 glass in the composite 
scaffolds. Based on the better capacity of 13-93B3 scaffolds to regenerate bone reported in one 
study [16], the borate 13-93B3 glass was designed to form the outer layer in the composite 
scaffolds. This meant that the 13-93B3 glass would be in apposition with the original (host) bone 
in the defect site. The scaffolds in all four groups were prepared with a final external diameter of 
4.6 mm, the diameter of the rat calvarial defects used in the in vivo experiments.  
The creation of the 13-93 and 13-93B3 scaffolds is described in detail elsewhere [11, 18]. 
Briefly, the as-received glass was crushed in a steel shatterbox (8500 Shatterbox®, Spex 
SamplePrep LLC, Metuchen, NJ) and sieved to give particles of size <53 μm. Then the particles 
of each glass were ground for 2.5 h in an attrition mill (Model 01-HD, Union Process, Akron, 
OH) using ZrO2 grinding media (3 mm in diameter) and water (for the 13-93 glass) or ethanol 
(for the 13-93B3 glass) as the liquid medium. The average particle size of each glass was 2 μm.

An aqueous-based paste composed of 40 vol% glass particles and 20 wt% aqueous 
Pluronic® F-127 solution was used to form the silicate 13-93 scaffolds. Because of the higher 
reactivity of the 13-93B3 glass, an organic-based paste composed of 40 vol% glass particles, 
12.8 vol% ethyl cellulose (EC; Acros Organics), 4.3 vol% poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) (PEG 
300; Alfa Aesar, Ward Hill, MA) and 42.9 vol% ethanol was used to prepare the 13-93B3 
scaffolds. The paste was loaded into a robocasting device (RoboCAD 3.0; 3D Inks, Stillwater, 
OK) and deposited on an Al2O3 substrate. Scaffolds with a grid-like microstructure were formed 
by extruding the paste through a 410 μm nozzle (EFD precision tips, East Providence, RI) with a 
center-to-center distance between the filaments of 910 μm. The scaffolds were dried for 24 h at 
room temperature, heated at 0.5 ºC/min to 600 ºC (13-93 glass) or to 500 ºC (13-93B3 glass) in 
flowing O2 gas to burn out the processing additives, and sintered for 1 h at 700 C (13-93 glass) 
or for 1 h at 560 C (13-93B3 glass) (heating rate = 5 C/min) to densify the glass filaments.  
Composite scaffolds composed of a mixture of 13-93 and 13-93B3 glass were prepared as 
follows. Grid-like 13-93 constructs were first created using the procedure described above but 
with diameters of 3.3 mm and 3.8 mm after sintering. Then 13-93B3 constructs (diameter = 8 
mm) were formed, dried and thermally treated to burn out the processing additives as described 
above. The resulting constructs were infiltrated with camphene (Sigma; St. Louis, MO) and a 
cylindrical cavity (~3.3 or ~3.8 mm) was drilled in the axial direction of the constructs. After 
subliming the camphene in a fume hood overnight, the tubular 13-93B3 constructs were placed 
around the 13-93 glass scaffolds and the composite constructs were sintered for 1 h at 560 C 
(heating rate = 5 C/min) to densify the 13-93B3 glass filaments and to bond the 13-93B3 layer 
to the 13-93 phase. The two groups of scaffolds were composed of (1) an inner region of 13-93 
glass of diameter 3.8 mm and an outer layer of 13-93B3 glass of thickness 0.4 mm (designated 
“thin” 13-93B3 layer) and (2) an inner region of 13-93 glass of diameter 3.3 mm and an outer 
layer of 13-93B3 glass of thickness 0.65 mm (designated “thick” 13-93B3 layer). The glass 
phase in the scaffolds was composed of 67 vol% 13-93 and 33 vol% 13-93B3 (thin 13-93B3 
layer) and 50 vol% 13-93 and 50 vol% 13-93B3 (thick 13-93B3 layer). 
 
Characterization of as-fabricated scaffolds 
The porosity of the scaffolds was measured using the Archimedes method according to a 
standard procedure (ASTM C830), using water as the liquid medium. The microstructure of the 
scaffolds was examined in a field-emission scanning electron microscope, FE-SEM (S-4700, 
Hitachi, Tokyo, Japan) at an accelerating voltage of 15 kV and a working distance of 12 mm. 
Scaffolds composed of 13-93 and 13-93B3 alone with a cubic shape (6 mm × 6 mm × 6 mm) 
and composite scaffolds composed of 13-93 and 13-93B3 glass with a cylindrical shape (8 mm 
in diameter × 6 mm) were tested in an Instron testing machine (Model 4881; Instron, Norwood, 
56
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
In Vivo Evaluation of Scaffolds with a Grid-Like Microstructure
MA) to measure their compressive strength and elastic modulus. Testing was performed at a 
deformation rate of 0.5 mm/min using a 10 kN load cell. Prior to the tests, the surfaces of the 
samples were ground using a surface grinder (FSG-618, Chevalier Machinery Inc., Santa Fe 
Springs, CA) to produce parallel contact surfaces. The load was applied to the samples in the 
direction perpendicular to the plane of robotic deposition (z direction). Five samples in each 
group were tested, and the data are expressed as a mean ± standard deviation (SD).  
 
Animals and surgical procedure 
All animal experimental procedures were approved by the Animal Care and Use Committee, 
Missouri University of Science and Technology, in compliance with the NIH Guide for Care and 
Use of Laboratory Animals (1985). The four groups of scaffolds were sterilized and implanted 
for 6 and 12 weeks in defects (4.6 mm in diameter) created in rat calvariae using a procedure 
described previously [17]. Briefly, scaffolds (4.6 mm in diameter × 1.5 mm) were washed three 
times with ethanol, dried at 60 ºC, and dry-heat sterilized for 12 h at 250 ºC. Twenty male 
Sprague Dawley rats (3 months old; weight = 350ࡁ400 g, Harlan Laboratories Inc., USA) were 
used in the experiments. The rats were pre-anesthetized with a combination of CO2 and air, and 
then anesthetized by intraperitoneal injection with a combination of ketamine (7 mg/kg) and 
xylazine (0.7 mg/kg). Using sterile instruments and aseptic technique, bilateral full-thickness 
defects 4.6 mm in diameter were created in the central area of each parietal bone using a 
saline-cooled trephine drill. The sites were constantly irrigated with sterile PBS to prevent 
overheating of the bone margins and to remove the bone debris. The bilateral defects were 
randomly implanted with 10 implants per group. Each animal received an intramuscular injection 
of ~200 μl penicillin and ~200 μl buprenorphine post-surgery. The animals were monitored daily. 
At 6 and 12 weeks, the animals were sacrificed by CO2 inhalation, and the calvarial defect sites 
with surrounding bone and soft tissue were harvested for subsequent evaluation. 
 
Histological processing 
The calvarial samples, including the defect sites with surrounding bone and tissue, were 
fixed in 10% buffered formaldehyde for 7 days and cut in half. Half of each sample was for 
paraffin embedding and the other half for methyl methacrylate embedding. The samples for 
paraffin embedding were de-siliconized by immersion in 10% hydrofluoric acid for 2 h, 
decalcified in 14% ethylendiaminetetracetic acid (EDTA) solution for 4 weeks, dehydrated in a 
series of graded ethanol, and embedded in paraffin using routine histological techniques. Then 
the specimens were sectioned to 5 μm and stained with hematoxylin and eosin (H&E). The 
undecalcified samples were dehydrated in ethanol and embedded in PMMA. Sections were cut to 
a thickness of ~ 200 μm using a microtome, affixed to acrylic slides, ground down to 30ࡁ40 μm 
using a surface grinder (EXAKT 400CS, Norderstedt, Germany), and stained using the von 
Kossa technique. Transmitted light images of the stained sections were taken with an Olympus 
BX 50 microscope connected with a CCD camera (DP70, Olympus, Japan). 
 
Histomorphometric analysis 
Histomorphometric analysis was carried out using optical images of the stained sections and 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
57
In Vivo Evaluation of Scaffolds with a Grid-Like Microstructure
the ImageJ software (National Institutes of Health, USA). The percent new bone formed in the 
defect sites implanted with the scaffolds was determined from the H&E stained sections. The 
entire defect area was defined as the area between the defect margins, including the entire glass 
scaffold and the tissue within. The newly formed bone was then outlined and measured. 
Quantitation of the von Kossa-positive stained area was performed using light micrographs of 
von Kossa-stained sections. The black-stained areas were outlined, measured and expressed as a 
fraction of the total defect area. Since the total black-stained area gave the mineralized bone area 
plus HA due to the glass conversion, the percent new bone determined from the H&E stained 
sections was subtracted from the total von Kossa positive area to determine the percent glass 
conversion. For each implantation time, five samples for each group were evaluated, and the 
results are presented as a mean ± SD.  
 
Statistical analysis 
Statistical analysis for differences between groups was performed using one-way analysis of 
variance (ANOVA) with Tukey’s post hoc test. Differences were considered significant for p < 
0.05.  
 
RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
 
Characteristics of bioactive glass scaffolds 
Figure 1aࡁd shows optical images of the top surface of the four groups of scaffolds as 
fabricated: 13-93 alone, 13-93 with a thin layer of 13-93B3 ( 33% 13-93B3), 13-93 with a thick 
layer of 13-93B3 ( 50% 13-93B3), and 13-93B3 alone. The 13-93 and 13-93B3 glass in all four 
scaffolds had the same grid-like microstructure shown in Fig. 1e, f, consisting of dense glass 
filaments (diameter = 330 ± 10 μm) and pores of width 300 ± 10 μm in the plane of deposition 
and 150 ± 10 μm in the direction perpendicular to the deposition plane. The porosity of the 
grid-like structure was 47 ± 1%. The darker color of the borate 13-93B3 glass phase in the 
scaffolds resulted from processing in an organic medium to avoid reaction with an aqueous 
medium. While the organic processing additives were burnt off as completely as possible, trace 
amounts of carbonaceous residue can lead to darkening of the glass after sintering. 
The compressive strength and elastic modulus of the scaffolds were dependent on the 
volume fraction of the borate 13-93B3 glass in the scaffold (Fig. 2). Because the strength of the 
BࡁO bond is weaker than that of the SiࡁO bond, the borate 13-93B3 glass has a lower 
compressive strength and elastic modulus than the silicate 13-93 glass, as observed previously 
[18, 19]. Consequently, for the geometry of the scaffolds used in this study, the strength and 
elastic modulus decreased with increasing volume fraction of 13-93B3 glass. Because of the 
lower strength of the 13-93B3 glass, the applied stress on the composite was supported mainly 
by the stronger 13-93 phase. The compressive strength and elastic modulus of the scaffolds 
decreased from 88  7 MPa and 10  1 GPa, respectively, for the 13-93 scaffolds to 28  11 MPa 
and 5  2 GPa, respectively, for the 13-93B3 scaffolds.

Assessment of bone regeneration in vivo 
The capacity of the scaffolds to regenerate bone in the defects was assessed from images of 
the H&E stained sections. Figure 3 shows transmitted light images of H&E stained sections of 
the rat calvarial defects implanted with the four groups of scaffolds at 12 weeks. New bone filled 
a large fraction of the pore space of the 13-93 scaffolds and bridged the defect (Fig. 3a). For the 
defects implanted with the composite scaffolds containing a thin or thick 13-93B3 layer, new 
bone infiltrated the 13-93B3 layer as well as the 13-93 inner region (Fig. 3b, c). In comparison, 
new bone formation was limited to the periphery of the scaffolds composed of 13-93B3 glass

sections at 6 weeks are omitted for brevity.

Assessment of mineralized bone and scaffold conversion to HA in vivo 
Optical images of von Kossa-stained sections of the defects implanted with the four groups 
of scaffolds at 12 weeks are shown in Fig. 5. The total von Kossa-positive area (the dark-stained 
area) within the defect indicated the presence of phosphate materials due to mineralized bone and 
to HA due to conversion of the bioactive glass scaffolds. The 13-93B3 glass appeared to be fully 
converted to HA within 6 weeks (results not shown). In comparison, the 13-93 glass was only 
partially converted at 12 weeks. 
 
Fig. 5. Optical images of von Kossa-stained sections of rat calvarial defects implanted with the 
four groups of scaffolds at 12 weeks: (a) 13-93; (b) 13-93 with thin layer of 13-93B3; (c) 13-93 
with thick layer of 13-93B3; (d) 13-93B3. Scale bar = 1 mm.  
 
For each group of scaffolds, the average total von Kossa-positive area, normalized to the 
total defect area, increased as the implantation time increased from 6 to 12 weeks but the 
difference was not significant (Fig. 6a). At 6 weeks, the defects implanted with the 13-93B3 
scaffold had a significantly higher total von Kossa positive area than the defects implanted with 
13-93 scaffold and with the composite scaffold composed of 13-93 glass with a thin layer of

·

In Vivo Evaluation of Scaffolds with a Grid-Like Microstructure
13-93B3. There was no significant difference among the four groups at 12 weeks.  
In order to assess the von Kossa-positive area due to the conversion of the scaffolds to HA 
(Fig. 6b), the percent new bone formed in the defect, determined from the H&E stained sections 
was subtracted from the total von Kossa-positive area. For each group of scaffolds, the von 
Kossa positive area due to the glass conversion showed little increase as the implantation time 
increased from 6 weeks to 12 weeks. This can be explained in terms of the complete conversion 
of the 13-93B3 glass by 6 weeks and the slow conversion of the 13-93 glass with time. The von 
Kossa-positive area due to the conversion of the 13-93B3 scaffold was significantly higher than 
the values for the other three groups at 6 weeks and significantly higher than the 13-93 scaffold 
at 12 weeks. The von Kossa-positive area due to the conversion of the 13-93B3 scaffold was 50 
 13% at 6 weeks and 51  10% at 12 weeks. These values are comparable to the volume 
fraction of the glass phase (53  1%) in the as-fabricated scaffolds, confirming the complete 
conversion of the 13-93B3 glass within 6 weeks. In comparison, the von Kossa-positive area due 
to the conversion of the 13-93 scaffold was 12  2% at 6 weeks and 15  5% at 12 weeks. 
   
 
Fig. 6. (a) Percent total von Kossa-positive area and (b) percent von Kossa-positive area with 
new bone subtracted in rat calvarial defects implanted with the four groups of scaffolds at 6 and 
12 weeks: 13-93, 13-93 with a thin layer of 13-93B3 (thin layer), 13-93 with a thick layer of 
13-93B3 (thick layer), and 13-93B3. *Significant difference between groups (p<0.05). 
 
The results of this study for the conversion of the scaffolds to HA are in agreement with the 
results of previous in vitro and in vivo studies that showed a more rapid degradation and 
conversion of borate 13-93B3 glass when compared to silicate 13-93 glass [14ࡁ17]. The in vivo 
results for bone regeneration obtained in this study are different from the results of a previous 
study [16] that showed a better capacity of 13-93B3 glass to support bone renegeration in rat 
calvarial defects when compared to 13-93 scaffolds. However, the results of the present study 
confirm those of a previous study [17] which showed a decreasing capacity of the scaffolds to 
regenerate bone with increasing amount of 13-93B3 glass in the scaffold. In those two previous 
studies [16, 17], the scaffolds has a fibrous microstructure composed of a random arrangement of 
thermally-bonded short glass fibers. 
The scaffolds in the present study had the same grid-like microstructure. In particular, the 
62
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
In Vivo Evaluation of Scaffolds with a Grid-Like Microstructure
microstructure of the 13-93 and 13-93B3 scaffolds was identical (Fig. 1). Consequently, 
differences in microstructure cannot account for the lower capacity of the 13-93B3 scaffolds to 
regenerate bone observed in this study. The decrease in bone regeneration with increasing 
13-93B3 glass in the scaffold (Fig. 4) suggests that the borate glass might have a reduced 
capacity to regenerate bone. One factor that might account for the lower capacity of the 13-93B3 
scaffolds to regenerate bone is toxicity resulting from high concentrations of boron released from 
the scaffolds. The fast degradation of the 13-93B3 glass leads to rapid release of high 
concentrations of boron in vivo. This could adversely affect cell viability initially which could 
have an effect on subsequent cellular and tissue reactions and, eventually, on bone formation.  
The processing steps used to create the grid-like 13-93 and 13-93B3 scaffolds were not 
identical. Because of the low reactivity of 13-93 glass, the paste used for robotic deposition of 
the 13-93 scaffolds was prepared using an aqueous Pluronic F-127 solution. In comparison, an 
organic-based paste containing ethanol and ethyl cellulose was used for creating the 13-93B3 
scaffolds in order to prevent degradation of the glass. While the subsequent thermal treatment of 
the scaffolds was performed to remove these organic additives as completely as possible, 
inevitably trace amounts of carbonaceous residue resulted in the 13-93B3 scaffolds having a 
darker color than the 13-93 scaffolds (Fig. 1). It is unclear whether the trace amount of 
carbonaceous residue in the 13-93B3 glass could be toxic to cells and tissues. However, the 
similarity in the trends for bone regeneration between this study and a previous study [17] in 
which fibrous scaffolds were fabricated by thermal bonding (without additives) indicates that 
trace amounts of carbonaceous residue in the 13-93B3 glass were not a major factor.        
 
CONCLUSIONS 
Bioactive glass scaffolds with a grid-like microstructure, composed of silicate 13-93 glass 
with varying amounts of borate 13-93B3 glass (0ࡁ100%) were created by robotic deposition and 
evaluated in vitro and in vivo. The compressive strength and elastic modulus of the as-fabricated 
scaffolds decreased with increasing amount of 13-93B3 glass in the scaffolds. When implanted 
in rat calvarial defects in vivo, the scaffolds showed a decreasing capacity to regenerate bone 
with increasing volume fraction of 13-93B3 glass in the scaffolds. The percent new bone in the 
defects implanted with the 13-93 scaffolds (23 ± 4 % at 6 weeks and 28 ± 8% at 12 weeks) was 
significantly higher than in the defects implanted with the 13-93B3 scaffolds (6 ± 4 % at 6 weeks 
and 9 ± 7 % at 12 weeks). While the 13-93 glass was only partially converted to HA after 12 
weeks in vivo, the 13-93B3 glass was almost fully converted within 6 weeks. The lower capacity 
of the 13-93B3 scaffolds to support bone regeneration might be related to the high boron 
concentration released from the rapidly-converting glass.

65
OSTEOCONDUCTIVE AND OSTEOINDUCTIVE IMPLANTS COMPOSED OF HOLLOW 
HYDROXYAPATITE MICROSPHERES 
 
Mohamed N. Rahaman1,*, Wei Xiao1, Yongxing Liu1, and B. Sonny Bal2 
1Department of Materials Science and Engineering and Center for Biomedical Science and 
Engineering, Missouri University of Science and Technology, Rolla, Missouri 65409, USA 
2Department of Orthopaedic Surgery, University of Missouri, Columbia, Missouri 65211, USA  
*Corresponding author (rahaman@mst.edu) 
  
ABSTRACT 
The repair of bone defects resulting from trauma, periodontal disease and congenital 
diseases is a common clinical problem. Autogenous bone grafts are the gold standard for 
treatment and allografts are alternatives but they suffer from limitations. Synthetic bone graft 
substitutes are typically osteoconductive but they lack the osteogenic cells and osteoinductive 
growths factors present in autologous bone. Consequently, they commonly require the addition 
of cells or growth factors to enhance bone regeneration. However, concerns have been expressed 
about the supraphysiological doses of bone morphogenetic proteins (such as BMP2) required for 
bone regeneration at a clinically acceptable rate. We have been developing hollow hydroxy- 
apatite (HA) microspheres as a bioactive and osteoconductive carrier for BMP2 in bone 
regeneration. The microspheres have a customizable diameter, surface area, hollow core and 
mesoporous shell wall. When loaded with BMP2, the HA microspheres showed a significant 
capacity to induce bone growth in rat calvarial defects within 3 to 6 weeks. Removing a segment 
of the shell from the hollow HA microspheres resulted in the creation of “open” microspheres 
that showed the interior concave surface of the shell wall. The open HA microspheres showed 
osteoinductive-like properties based on the microconcave geometry of the interior surface of the 
shell wall. In this article, the characteristics of the hollow HA micro- spheres, their ability to 
serve as a carrier for BMP2 and their capacity to regenerate bone in a standard osseous defect 
model are reviewed. These hollow HA microspheres, closed or open, are promising 
osteoconductive and osteoinductive implants for regenerating bone in periodontal, craniofacial 
and non-loaded musculoskeletal defects. 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Bone defects resulting from trauma, malignancy, infection, and congenital disease are a 
common occurrence in orthopaedic, craniofacial and dental practice [1]. Clinically, these defects 
can be reconstructed through the use of various bone grafts such as autologous bone grafts, 
allografts and biocompatible synthetic biomaterials [1ࡁ3]. Autologous bone grafts, the gold 
standard for treatment, heal reliably as they contain osteoinductive growth factors and osteogenic 
cells and they provide a structural scaffold. However, autografts suffer from problems such as 
limited tissue availability, increased surgery time, additional pain and cosmetic imperfection at 
the donor site [4, 5]. Allogenic bone, the most widely used bone graft, can induce moderate 
healing due to its preserved osteoinductivity. However, allografts are costly, have unpredictable 
healing to bone and adverse immune reactions, and they increase the risk of disease transference 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
66
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
[6]. Synthetic bone grafts have advantages such as unlimited supply, consistent quality, safety 
and good tissue tolerance but they commonly function merely as bioinert or osteoconductive 
implants [1ࡁ3, 7ࡁ11]. Although encouraging results have been reported [10ࡁ12], the use of 
synthetic bone grafts by themselves commonly fails to produce sufficient bone formation in a 
clinically relevant time [11, 13]. Adding autologous bone [11, 13ࡁ15] or osteogenic growth 
factors [16, 17] to synthetic grafts is often needed to achieve reliable reconstruction of bone.  
 
Osteogenic bone morphogenetic proteins (BMPs) can induce robust bone formation [18]. 
BMPs such as (BMP2) have been used successfully in orthopedic repair and their applications in 
periodontal and craniofacial bone regeneration are being actively investigated [19ࡁ21]. BMPs 
are commonly delivered by biodegradable or osteoconductive carriers [3, 22ࡁ24], such as 
collagen, biodegradable synthetic polymers such as poly(lactic acid) (PLA), poly(glycolic acid) 
PGA and their copolymers (PLGA), calcium phosphate bioceramics such as hydroxyapatite 
(HA), beta-tricalcium phosphate ( -TCP) and biphasic calcium phosphate (BCP), and bioactive 
glasses. The BMP release kinetics from the carrier and the retention of the BMP biological 
activity are critically important for successful bone induction [18]. Carriers with good 
osteoconductive and bioactive properties are also desirable for bone repair as they bond directly 
to the original (host) bone [18]. 
Collagen has been used for several years as a carrier for BMP2 in orthopedic practice [1ࡁ3]. 
However, collagen is not osteoconductive and collagen carriers release the bulk of their BMPs 
initially, followed by a more gradual release thereafter [25]. Consequently, BMPs are often 
effective only when used in supraphysiological doses in collagen which has resulted in 
heightened concerns about adverse biological effects, such as greater risk of malignancy, ectopic 
bone formation and osteolysis [26ࡁ29]. The reduction of a high initial dose could make the use 
of BMPs clinically safer. Decreasing the typical initial burst release and extending the sustained 
release of BMPs by improved design of the carrier is a desirable approach [23, 30]. The 
biodegradable polymers PLA, PGA and PLGA have been applied clinically in fracture fixation 
and they have been researched as carriers for BMPs because of the possibility for controlled 
release [3]. The degradation rate of those polymers and, therefore, the BMP release kinetics can 
be varied by changing their material properties such as composition and geometry. However, 
PLA, PGA and PLGA are not osteoconductive and there is a concern is that a large proportion of 
the BMP does not retain its activity after release from those carriers [31].    
 
The calcium phosphate bioceramics HA, -TCP and BCP have been used clinically in bone 
repair and augmentation since the 1980s [32ࡁ35]. They are bioactive and osteoconductive but 
they are generally not osteoinductive [36, 37]. One of the most emphasized differences between 
HA and -TCP is their relative degradation rates [38]. Synthetic HA is almost non-degradable 
while -TCP degrades (resorbs) far faster. The calcium phosphate ceramics have a strong affinity 
for binding and concentrating proteins which make them suitable carriers for growth factors such 
as BMP2 [36, 39]. Several studies have shown that the combination of porous particles, granules 
and scaffolds of calcium phosphate bioceramics with BMPs enhances osteogenesis in animal 
models when compared to the bioceramics alone [40ࡁ45]. However, the properties of the 
bioceramic such as porosity, pore size, pore interconnectivity and degradation rate can have a 
marked effect on osteogenesis. The non-resorbable property of synthetic HA can severely limit 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
67
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
BMP release and, therefore, the ability to stimulate osteogenesis [36, 46ࡁ49]. 
Calcium phosphate bioceramics such as HA, -TCP and BCP have been reported to have the 
ability to induce bone formation in non-bony sites without the addition of osteogenic growth 
factors [50ࡁ53]. Since the osteoinductivity was observed in some calcium phosphate bioceramics 
but not in others, these materials were described as having “intrinsic” osteoinductivity [37]. This 
osteoinductive property has been attributed to a variety of factors such as the chemical 
composition, surface topography, geometry and pore characteristics of the biomaterial [39, 
54ࡁ57]. Macroporosity, mesoporosity and concavity of the macropore surface appear to be 
important characteristics because they allow entrapment and concentration of circulating 
osteogenic growth factors (such as bone morphogenetic proteins, BMPs) and osteoprogenitor 
cells already present in the defect site in vivo. 
 
In the last few years, we have been developing a hollow HA microsphere technology to meet 
the need for improved synthetic bone graft substitutes [58ࡁ61]. The HA microspheres have a 
unique combination of properties that can be customized, such as microsphere and hollow core 
diameter, high surface area, mesoporous shell wall and degradation rate. By removing a segment 
of the shell from the hollow HA microspheres, we have created “open” HA microspheres that 
showed the interior concave surface of the shell wall. When necessary, the intact hollow HA 
microspheres are referred to as “closed” microspheres while the microspheres with a segment of 
the shell removed are referred to as “open” microspheres. In this article, the results of our 
previous studies on the preparation and characteristics of hollow HA microspheres, their ability 
serve as a carrier for BMP2 and their capacity to heal bone defects in a standard small animal 
model are reviewed. Then our recent results for the capacity of the open HA microspheres 
(without growth factors) to support the osteogenic differentiation of mesenchymal stem cells 
(MSCs) in vitro and to induce bone formation in vivo are discussed.      
  
PREPARATION OF HOLLOW HA MICROSPHERES 
Hollow HA microspheres are prepared by a novel glass conversion process near room 
temperature (Fig. 1), as described in detail elsewhere [58]. Briefly, solid microspheres of a borate 
glass (composition 15.0 CaO, 10.6 Li2O, 74.4 B2O3; wt%) are reacted in an aqueous phosphate 
(K2HPO4) solution. The temperature, concentration and pH of the solution influence the 
characteristics of the hollow HA microspheres [58]. The hollow microspheres described in this 
study were prepared at 37 C in a 0.02 M K2HPO4 solution with a pH = 9.0. Hollow HA 
microspheres of diameter 106ࡁ150 and 150ࡁ250 μm were commonly used in our previous 
studies but microspheres of diameter <100 μm to 1000 μm can be readily prepared. The 
reaction time varies from 2 days to 7 days, depending on the diameter of the starting glass 
microspheres (100ࡁ1000 μm). As the glass degrades, the released Ca2+ ions react with the 
phosphate ions from the solution to form an amorphous calcium phosphate (ACP) product on the 
surface of the microspheres [58]. Further reaction leads to the growth of the ACP layer inward 
and crystallization of the ACP phase to HA until the reaction is completed. The external diameter 
of the HA microspheres is equal to the original diameter of the starting glass microspheres but 
the diameter of the hollow core can be varied by changing the glass composition and the reaction 
conditions (such as the temperature and the concentration of the phosphate solution). 
68
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
 
Fig. 1. Schematic diagram showing the main steps in converting solid CaO-Li2O-B2O3 glass 
microspheres to hollow HA microspheres. 
               
 
  
Fig. 2. SEM images of (a) hollow HA microspheres (106ࡁ150 μm), and (b) the cross section of 
an HA microsphere showing the hollow core. (Inset: High magnification SEM image of surface 
of the nanostructured mesoporous HA shell. Bar = 1 μm). 
 
CHARACTERISTICS OF HOLLOW HA MICROSPHERES 
As-prepared hollow HA microspheres of external diameter 106ࡁ150 μm are described in this 
article. The microspheres have a nearly stoichiometric HA composition, a surface area of 102 
m2/g, a hollow core that is six-tenths the external diameter, a mesoporous shell (pore size = 
10ࡁ20 nm) and a rupture strength of 11  6 MPa. As described in detail elsewhere [59], the 
characteristics of the as-prepared microspheres can be modified by changing the reaction 
conditions and by using a thermal treatment. By adding varying amounts of carbonate ions to the 
phosphate solution used in the glass conversion process (Fig. 1), hollow microspheres composed 
of carbonate-substituted HA with controllable amounts of carbonate content (0 10 wt%) and, 
hence, degradation rate can be prepared. Heating the as-prepared HA microspheres above 200 
C results leads to changes in the microstructural characteristics of the HA shell. At temperatures 
of 500ࡁ600 C, the HA particles and the pores in the shell become coarser (but there is no 
measurable reduction in the total porosity), resulting in a reduction in the surface area (and, 
hence, the degradation rate) and an increase in the rupture strength. At higher temperatures, the 
HA particles continue to coarsen but the porosity of the shell decreases due to sintering, leading 
to a further decrease in the surface area and an increase in the rupture strength. Eventually at 
temperatures above 800ࡁ900 C, the shell becomes dense due to sintering. In general, the 
reaction conditions used in the glass conversion process and thermal treatment of the as-prepared 
microspheres can be used to customize the characteristics of the HA microspheres.

·

Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
HOLLOW HA MICROSPHERES AS A CARRIER FOR BMP2 
Hollow HA microspheres (106ࡁ150 μm) were loaded with BMP2 (Shenandoah Biotech- 
nology Inc., Warwick, PA) and used to study the release kinetics of BMP2 in vitro. The 
procedures are described in detail elsewhere [61]. Briefly, 10 mg of microspheres were placed in 
a 1 ml micro-centrifuge tube, and 10 μl of BMP2 solution (formed by dissolving 10 μg BMP2 in 
100 ml sterile citric acid at pH = 3.0) was pipetted on to the microspheres. A small vacuum was 
applied to replace the air in the microspheres with the BMP2 solution. Then, the BMP2- loaded 
microspheres were dried overnight at 4 C. Some of the BMP2-loaded HA microspheres were 
coated with a biodegradable polymer (PLGA) to modify the release of the BMP2 from the 
microspheres. In the coating process, 20 μl of a PLGA solution in chloroform was added to 10 
mg of BMP2-loaded HA microspheres in a centrifuge tube, and the system was dried at 4 C for 
24 h. Two different concentrations of the PLGA solutions were used (50 mg/ml and 200 mg/ml) 
to create coatings with different thicknesses. 
To measure the release on BMP2 from the microspheres, 500 μl of a sterile solution 
composed of equal volumes of fetal bovine serum (FBS) and phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) 
was added to the 10 mg microspheres in micro-centrifuge tubes and the samples were incubated 
at 37 C. At selected times, the FBS/PBS solution was removed for testing, and replaced with 
fresh solution. Control samples containing a known amount of BMP2 in a buffer were also 
incubated at 37 C. The amount of BMP2 present in the releasing medium was measured using 
an ELISA kit.  
 
Fig. 3. Cumulative amount of BMP2 released from hollow HA microspheres into an FBS/PBS 
buffer medium in vitro as a function of time. The data are shown for the uncoated HA 
microspheres loaded with BMP2, and the BMP2-loaded microspheres coated with solutions 
composed of 50 mg/ml PLGA and 200 mg/ml. (From Ref. 61) 
 
 
Figure 3 shows the average cumulative amount of BMP2 released from the uncoated and 
PLGA- coated HA microspheres into the medium. The uncoated microspheres showed an initial 
“burst” release at 1 h, followed by a more continuous release at longer times. In comparison, 
there appeared to be a delay in the release of BMP2 from the microspheres coated with the 
PLGA solution. The amount of BMP2 released from the coated microspheres at 1 h was 
70
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
significantly smaller than that for the uncoated microspheres but it became higher at day 1 and 
day 3. Thereafter, the amount of BMP2 released was lower or comparable to that for the 
uncoated microspheres. The cumulative amount of BMP2 released at any time decreased with 
increasing amount of the PLGA coating.  
A distinct feature of the results was the low amount of BMP2 released from the 
microspheres. When the experiments were terminated after 14 days, the cumulative amount of 
BMP2 released, determined as a fraction of the amount initially loaded into the microspheres, 
was 1.8%, 1.5%, and 1.0%, respectively, for the uncoated microspheres, the microspheres coated 
with the 50 mg/ml solution and the microspheres coated with the 200 mg/ml solution. BMP2 has 
a high affinity for HA and, consequently, its release rate from HA in vitro is often low. The 
adsorbed BMP2 is strongly immobilized on the surface of the HA by electrostatic and other 
interactions (such as hydrogen bonding) which makes it difficult for the BMP2 to be displaced 
from the HA surface by competitive adsorption from other proteins [62ࡁ64]. However, the 
amount released from the hollow HA microspheres used in this study was still lower than those 
observed from more conventional HA materials [63, 64].  
While a few factors can contribute to the low BMP2 release rate, the unique structural 
features of the hollow HA microspheres, such as the high-surface-area mesoporous shell, might 
be the most important. The amount of BMP2 initially loaded into the microspheres was <1% of 
the theoretical amount of BMP2 that can be adsorbed onto the surface area of the HA 
microspheres used in this study [64]. Becuase of the strong affinity of HA for BMP2, the 
desorbed BMP2 could be readily re-adsorbed on the largely free HA surface, which would 
markedly reduce the amount of BMP2 released into the medium. The tortuous mesopores of the 
HA shell could also make the migration of BMP2 from the hollow core or from the pores to the 
external surface difficult because of the large surface area available for adsorption.  
A higher release rate of BMP2 from the microspheres can be expected in vivo because of a 
higher degradation rate of the HA due to cell-mediated degradation in addition to 
dissolution-mediated degradation [65, 66] and the higher solubility of proteins in vivo. If 
required, the release of BMP2 from the as-prepared microspheres can be improved by increasing 
the dissolution (degradation) rate of the HA which is dependent on the composition and structure 
of the HA [38]. The HA microspheres used in these studies had a nearly stoichiometric 
composition and, consequently, a low degradation rate. Recently, we have prepared hollow HA 
microspheres with varying amounts of carbonate substitution which show a higher degradation 
rate. Those microspheres are being evaluated in vitro and in vivo.    
The rate at which the BMP2 is released with time is relevant to the use of these hollow HA 
microspheres as a carrier in bone regeneration. In particular, a high initial burst release of BMP2 
has often been observed from HA carrier materials [67]. A burst release of BMP2 can induce 
inflammatory reactions which are believed to be beneficial for promoting bone formation, but it 
can also cause significant adverse biological effects. While a reduction in the BMP2 dose 
delivered in vivo could reduce those side effects, a high dose is often required to induce 
significant bone formation [26, 68]. 
Coating the BMP2-loaded microspheres with PLGA is effective in reducing the amount of 
BMP2 released during the first hour when compared to the uncoated microspheres (Fig. 3) [61]. 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
71
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
The amount of BMP2 released from the coated microspheres was 4 to 5 times lower than that for 
the uncoated microspheres. Subsequently, the amount of BMP2 released from the microspheres 
coated with the 50 mg/ml PLGA solution increased, and the cumulative amount of BMP2 
released was not significantly different from that of the uncoated microspheres. In comparison, 
the amount of BMP2 released from the microspheres coated with the 200 mg/ml solution 
remained at a much lower value, presumably because the larger PLGA coverage provided a 
greater barrier to the release of BMP2. The results showed the feasibility of combining the 
hollow HA microspheres with a biodegradable polymer coating to control the BMP2 release 
profile. However, it is expected that optimization of the coating will depend on the clinical 
application. For example, the use of a much higher BMP2 dose (1500 ug/ml), as in some clinical 
applications [69], will require the coating thickness to be adjusted accordingly in order to 
achieve optimum control of the release profile.  
 
HOLLOW HA MICROSPHERES AS IMPLANTS FOR BONE REGENERATION 
Hollow HA microspheres were implanted in calvarial defects (4.6 mm) of Sprague Dawley 
rats to evaluate their capacity to support bone regeneration [61]. Implants composed of 
microspheres (diameter = 106ࡁ150 μm) without BMP2 and microspheres loaded with BMP2 (1 
μg/defect) were evaluated. At 6 weeks, the calvarial samples, including the defect sites with 
surrounding bone and tissue, were sectioned and stained with hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) and 
the von Kossa technique using routine histological techniques. Histomorphometric analysis was 
carried out using optical images of the stained sections and the ImageJ software (National 
Institutes of Health, USA). The percent new bone formed in the defect sites implanted with the 
scaffolds was determined from the H&E stained sections as a fraction of the total defect area.  
  
Fig. 4. H&E stained sections of rat calvarial defect implanted for 6 weeks with (a) hollow HA 
microspheres (106 150 m), and (b) hollow HA microspheres loaded with BMP2 (1 g/defect). 
(HB = host bone; NB = new bone). (From Ref. 61) 
 
The majority of the new bone formed in the defects implanted with the hollow HA 
microspheres (without growth factor) was osteoconductive in nature. New bone infiltrated the 
pores between the microspheres from the edge of the defect and grew along the dural side of the 
defect (Fig. 4a). Loading the hollow HA microspheres (106ࡁ150 μm) with BMP2 was effective 
in stimulating osteoinductive bone formation (Fig. 4b). New bone formed within the pores of the 
microspheres and bridged the defect within the six-week implantation period. The percent new 
formed in the defects implanted with the BMP2-loaded microspheres (43 
 6%) was 
significantly higher than that (17  10%) in the defects implanted with the microspheres without 
BMP2. The results indicate that these hollow HA microspheres are a promising osteoconductive 
72
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
carrier for BMP2 to stimulate osteoinductive bone growth. The BMP2 dose in the present study 
(1 μg/defect) was approximately in the mid-range of values used in other studies with this small 
animal model [70]. However, it is not clear whether a lower BMP2 dose can be equally effective 
in stimulating bone regeneration.  
 
CREATION OF OPEN HA MICROSPHERES 
Open HA microspheres have been created by grinding the hollow HA microspheres to 
remove a segment of the shell, as shown schematically in Fig. 5a. Hollow HA microspheres 
(106ࡁ150 μm), prepared as described earlier, were placed at the bottom of a plastic mold to form 
a single layer (e.g., by pouring the microspheres into the mold and vibrating the mold slightly). 
Then water was poured into the mold to a suitable depth to cover the microspheres and the 
system was frozen. After grinding the bottom surface of the frozen material on 1200 grit 
sandpaper in a cold chamber at 4 C, the microspheres were released from the ice by immersing 
the system in water at room temperature. The microspheres were collected, washed 3 times with 
water, then 3 times with ethanol, and dried for >12 hours at 90 C.  
 
 
 
Fig. 5. (a) Schematic diagram illustrating the method used for preparing open HA microspheres 
from (closed) hollow HA microspheres; (b) SEM image of a sample composed of closed 
microspheres and 25% and open microspheres (arrow). Small debris (arrowhead) present in the 
mixture sample can be removed by sieving. (Bar = 250 μm) 
  
The difference between the open and closed HA microspheres was geometrical in nature; the 
open HA microspheres showed the interior concave surface of the shell. Figure 5b shows an 
SEM image of HA microspheres composed of closed and open microspheres. The mixture was 
estimated to contain 25% open microspheres but mixtures with varying amounts of open 
microspheres (0ࡁ50%) have been created. Because of the size range of the closed microspheres 
(106ࡁ150 μm) used in this study, it was not possible to grind off the same amount of the shell 
from each microsphere. There are also residual HA debris in the mixture. Narrowing the size 
range of the starting hollow HA microspheres and sieving the mixture after the grinding step 
should improve the quality of the open HA microspheres formed by this method.  
 
OSTEOGENIC EFFECT OF OPEN HA MICROSPHERES IN VITRO 
 
The osteogenic effect of the open microspheres was assessed from their ability to support 
the differentiation of rat mesenchymal stem cells (rMSCs) in vitro. HA microspheres (106ࡁ150 
μm) containing 0, 25% and 50% open microspheres were used in order to determine the effect 
(a)
(b)
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
73
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
of the open microsphere content on cell differentiation. GIBCO® rMSCs (Sprague Dawley) (Life 
Technologies, NY) were propagated in maintaining medium composed of low-glucose 
Dulbecco's modified Eagle’s medium (DMEM) with GlutaMAX™-I, 10% MSC-qualified fetal 
bovine serum (FBS), 100 U/ml penicillin and 100 μg/ml streptomycin (Life Technologies, NY). 
Cell culture was performed in osteogenic medium composed of the maintaining medium 
supplemented with 50 μM magnesium L-ascorbic acid-phosphate and 10 mM beta-glycero- 
phosphate (both from Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO). No osteoinductive agents were added in 
order to distinguish the intrinsic osteogenic properties of the open HA microspheres [71, 72]. The 
cells (2 ×105) were seeded on 10 mg of HA microspheres placed in a 5 ml sterile polystyrene 
tube. (This mass of HA microspheres is the amount used in the in vivo experiments described 
later). Cells suspended in 100 μl culture medium were dropped onto the microspheres, gently 
mixed, allowed to attach for 3 h in a humidified CO2 incubator at 37 C. Then 3 ml of culture 
medium was gently added to each tube. The cells were cultured for 14 days (n = 3 per group), 
with half of the medium replaced with fresh medium every 3ࡁ4 days. 
 
Fig. 6. Alkaline phosphatase (ALP) activity of rat MSCs cultured for 14 days on hollow HA 
microspheres (106ࡁ150 μm) containing different amounts of open HA microspheres. 
 
 
Cell viability and proliferation was assessed using the CellTiter-Blue® Cell Viability Assay 
(Promega; Madison, WI) following the manufacturer’s protocol. Osteogenic differentiation of the 
cells was assessed from their alkaline phosphatase (ALP) activity, measured using a SensoLyte® 
pNPP Alkaline Phosphatase Assay Kit following the manufacturer’s manual (AnaSpec, Fremont, 
CA). The results (Fig. 6) showed that the open HA microspheres can promote osteogenesis. The 
rMSCs seeded on the microspheres containing 25% and 50% open HA microspheres showed 
significantly better ALP activity than the microspheres composed of closed HA microspheres 
only (control group). Furtherm0ore, the increase in ALP activity increased with the amount of 
the open HA microspheres. 
 
OSTEOINDUCTIVE-LIKE PROPERTIES OF OPEN HA MICROSPHERES IN VIVO   
 
To evaluate the capacity of the open HA microspheres to promote osteogenesis in vivo, 
hollow HA microspheres (106ࡁ150 μm) containing 0, 25% and 50% open microspheres were 
implanted in rat calvarial defects for 6 weeks. The animal surgery and histologic processing were 
74
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
the same as those described earlier for implants composed of closed HA microspheres. New 
bone formation in the defects implanted with the closed HA microspheres ( 0% open 
microspheres) was limited to the periphery (edge) and on the dural side of the defects, as 
described earlier (see Fig. 4a). Bone was rarely observed within the closed microspheres or in the 
pores between the closed microspheres. The closed microspheres were filled with unidentified 
materials that were stained weak pink, presumably extracellular matrix (ECM) proteins that 
penetrated the mesoporous shell and accumulated in the hollow core.  
 
 
Fig. 7. (a) Optical image of H&E stained section of rat calvarial defect implanted for 6 weeks 
with hollow HA microspheres containing 50% open microspheres. (b) Higher magnification 
image of the boxed area shown in (a).       
 
Defects implanted with the HA microspheres containing 25 and 50% open microspheres 
showed considerable bone formation in the many of the micro-concave regions of the open 
microspheres (Fig. 7). The amount of new bone within the microspheres apparently increased 
with increasing amount of open microspheres in the implants. The regions between the 
microspheres were filled with cell-rich connective tissues, similar to the tissues found between 
the closed HA microspheres. As these open HA microspheres have the same composition as the 
closed microspheres, their osteoinductive-like properties in vivo resulted essentially from the 
micro-concavity of their shell. As discussed previously [54ࡁ57], a micro-concave surface could 
allow entrapment and concentration of circulating osteogenic growth factors such as BMPs and 
osteoprogenitor cells already present in the defect site in vivo, resulting in the better capacity to 
induce new bone formation within the open HA microspheres. 
When compared to other intrinsically osteoinductive biomaterials reported in the literature, 
these open HA microspheres have the advantages of ease of fabrication, low cost, and reasonable 
large-scale production for routine clinical use. However, in common with other intrinsically 
osteoinductive biomaterials [71, 73], new bone formation is limited to within the micro-concave 
geometry. Consequently, the effectiveness of these open HA microspheres remains to be 
improved in order to achieve greater and more significant bone formation. 
 
CONCLUSIONS 
Hollow hydroxyapatite (HA) microspheres are showing promising results for use as implants 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
75
Osteoconductive and Osteoinductive Implants Composed of Hollow Hydroxyapatite
in bone repair. In addition to being bioactive and osteoconductive, hollow HA microspheres have 
an attractive combination of customizable properties such as high surface area, external and 
hollow core diameters, mesoporous shell wall and degradation rate. When loaded with BMP2, 
hollow HA microspheres have the capacity to induce significant bone formation in a standard 
osseous defect model within 3 to 6 weeks. Removing a segment of the shell from the hollow HA 
microspheres resulted in the creation of “open” microspheres that showed the microconcave 
interior surface of the shell. The open HA microspheres showed a significantly better ability to 
promote osteogenic differentiation of rat mesenchymal stem cells in vitro when compared to the 
“closed” HA microspheres. When implanted in rat calvarial defects in vivo, the open HA 
microspheres (without osteogenic growth factors or cells) showed osteoinductive-like properties. 
Considerable bone formation was found in the micro-concave regions of the open microspheres, 
presumably as a result of the concentration of local growth factors and osteoprogenitor cells 
within those micro-concave regions. These hollow HA microspheres, closed or open, are 
promising osteoconductive and osteoinductive implants for regenerating bone in periodontal, 
craniofacial and non-loaded musculoskeletal defects.
