where A is the projected contact area, S is the stiffness representing the slope of the 
initial unloading part of the curve, v is the Poisson’s ratio, and ͤ is a factor that depends on the 
indenter shape (ͤ = 1 here for a spherical indenter). The terms shown with subscript 0 and i are 
the properties of the test sample and indenter, respectively. A sensitivity study showed that the 
elastic modulus calculated from indentation depends little (e.g. variation < 8%) on the variation 
of Poisson’s ratio from 0.2-0.432. As a result, v0 = 0.31 of the fully dense LSCF material reported 
in13 was used in Equation (1) for all specimens. 
 
3D Microstructural Reconstruction 
An FIB/SEM dual beam instrument (Helios NanoLab 600i, FEI, USA) was used for the 
3D microstructural characterisation. The sintered LSCF film on the CGO substrate (LSCF/CGO 
pellet) was impregnated with epoxy resin under vacuum to enhance the grayscale contrast 
between pore phase and LSCF phase, and to improve the lateral resolution. The sample was then 
coated with a thin layer of gold to provide good electronic conductivity. A 2μm thick protective 
platinum layer was deposited on the surface of the cathode after the sample was put into the 
FIB/SEM vacuum chamber, in order to protect the microstructure and to prevent charging effect 
in the course of slicing and imaging. Prior to FIB slicing, it was necessary to machine a trench 
surrounding the three edges of the surface of interest in order to produce a peninsula-shaped 
114
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
volume of interest for sectioning.  A cross-shaped fiducial mark was then cut on one side of the 
volume of interest to allow positioning and distance check during working. Once the volume of 
interest was prepared and the sectioning parameters were defined, the serial cross-sectional 
milling by FIB and imaging of the resulting exposed surface by SEM was performed 
automatically by the instrument. The procedure is shown in detail in Figure 1. 
 
 
Figure 1. Steps in the 3D microstructural reconstruction of the LSCF cathode using FIB/SEM. In 
b) and c), black (grayscale value=0) corresponds to pores, white corresponds to LSCF material; d) 
shows the 3D reconstructed microstructure and e) after meshing for FEM. 
 
In the current study, the working volume of the LSCF film was approximately 10×20×6 
μm, with SEM image pixel dimensions of 12.5nm and slice thickness of 12.5nm. The acquired 
images were then realigned along the direction of serial sectioning and segmented to distinguish 
solid material and pores using image processing software Avizo Fire 6.0 (VSG Co. USA). A 
smaller volume of ~3×3×3 μm, consisting of 240 serial 2D images, was selected from the initial 
volume for 3D reconstruction. This resulted in a 3D microstructural dataset with voxel sizes of 
12.5x12.5x12.5nm. The data was then processed using ScanIP 6.9 software (Simpleware, UK) to 
generate tetrahedral meshes for the solid phase, to be used in the finite element modelling (FEM).  
 
 
 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
115
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
Finite Element Modelling (FEM) 
FEM was performed using Abaqus CAE 6.10 (Dassault Systemes, USA) to calculate the 
effective elastic modulus of the meshed 3D microstructure. In the analysis, the elastic modulus of 
the fully dense LSCF pellet determined using nanoindentation and Poisson’s ratio of 0.31 from 13 
were used as the material property inputs of the solid content of the FE model. The analysis was 
run using the Abaqus Standard FE solver based on the assumption that the LSCF material was 
isotropic, linear and elastic. A small displacement was applied on one free surface normal to the 
Y direction so that the model deformed linearly along Y. The opposite face was constrained to 
have no displacement in this direction. Boundary conditions were also applied to constrain the 
degree of freedom of the normal displacement for the nodes on the model’s two adjacent 
surfaces parallel to Y. Such settings allowed the rest two surfaces to contract or extend freely 
once the displacement was applied. The resultant average force on the displaced surface was 
obtained from the model in order to calculate the effective elastic modulus of the 3D 
microstructure. The dimensionless Poisson’s ratio was calculated from the ratio of the transverse 
and longitudinal strains of the surfaces. 
 
RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
 
Nanoindentation 
As mentioned earlier, nanoindentation measurements of porous films deposited on 
substrates are much more complicated than that of bulk samples due to more factors affecting the 
indentation behaviour. Surface roughness was inherent to the processing method of our films and 
could be less problematic due to the use of spherical tips and diluted ink. Crack-free films with 
macroscopically flat surface were fabricated as shown in Figure 2. 
 
 
Figure 2. (a) Sintered LSCF film on CGO substrate pellet; SEM images of (b) cross-section of 
the sample and (c) the top surface of the film. 
 
However, there existed effects from substrate upon indentation approaching the 
film/substrate interface, which would be expected to affect the experimental data. Consequently 
the estimated elastic modulus of the sample could not be obtained straightforwardly from the 
nanoindentation data as the measured modulus varied with the indentation depth, but could only 
be obtained by removing those effects when applying extrapolation based on the test data.  
Figure 3 shows the nanoindentation data of elastic modulus as a function of maximum 
indentation depth relative to film thickness ratio (i.e. hmax/tf) of films after sintering at 900-
1200 °C. Further data analysis will be discussed afterwards.

·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
 
Figure 3. Elastic modulus vs. hmax/tf ratio for porous films after sintering at (a)-(d): 900-1200 °C 
 
The result indicates a clear dependence of measured elastic modulus on normalised 
indentation depth, which was largely controlled by the combined effects from surface roughness, 
densification and substrate.  
It can be seen from the result that, the scatter for shallow indent depths less than 10% of 
the film thickness is particularly large. This is due to the surface roughness. These shallow 
indentation depths are comparable to the grain size (270 nm) and the surface roughness (Ra > 
402 nm for 1000 °C sintered films) and result in variability in the initial stages on indentation. A 
cross section through a shallow indent is shown in Figure 4 together with the outline of the 
indenter tip where it contacts the surface. It can be seen that hmax in this case is of similar 
magnitude to the surface roughness and that the contact is at asperities on the specimen surface. 
The stresses at these points are much greater than for ideal contact between two smooth surfaces 
and the displacements are greater.

Figure 4. Effect of surface roughness on the real contact area with the indenter tip, leading to 
errors in indentation results. Here the sample shown was sintered at 1000 °C 
 
Due to the facts presented above, surface roughness might have two main effects on the 
measured results. One was the underestimation of E at shallow indentation depth, as shown in 
Figure 3. This underestimation was due to the overestimated contact area, provided that the 
indentation depth was comparable to the roughness, as also reported by other investigations 33,34. 
The measured E increased with the indentation depth as the surface roughness effect gradually 
diminished when an increasing number of the untouched valleys close to the asperities on the 
surface under compression were touched by the indenter tip. Such effect can be eliminated in 
data analysis by simply discarding the indentation depths smaller than a critical value, for which 
the result are less reliable, as suggested by Mencik and Swain35. Therefore, for the films studied 
in our case, indenting deeper than 10% of the film thickness was a prerequisite to generate 
reasonable values close to the film-only properties. Besides, surface roughness could also be 
responsible for the scatter in the measured data over the whole range of indentation depth as 
shown in Figure 3. Nevertheless, the standard deviations indicate that for the indentation depth in 
the range of 0.1-0.2tf, the elastic modulus data were more consistent and reproducible than that 
for the other depths. This was particularly true for films sintered at 900 and 1000 °C. While for 
the films sintered at 1100 and 1200 °C, the errors were much larger, which were attributed to the 
much coarser surface features after sintering at higher temperature. Nevertheless, no significant 
cracks were found in the indented region. Another observation which can be made from the SEM 
images is that the common pile-up and sink-in effects (as shown in Figure 5) of the film material 
affected by the substrate 36 are not found here.  
 
 
Figure 5. Schematic of half-space cross-sectional view of the effect of separated event of pile-up 
and sink-in on the actual contact area for nanoindentation on films with a spherical indenter 
118
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
Often the soft films deposited on hard substrates have the possibility to pile-up upon 
indentation due to the change of the films’ plastic deformation behaviour attributed to the 
constraint stress enforced by the relatively harder substrate, whereas in the opposite case the 
films tend to sink-in. As a result, the former case could erroneously increase the mechanical 
properties calculated using Oliver-Pharr method because according to the method E 
 A-1/2 
where A is extracted from nanoindentation load-depth data and not from direct measurement, 
while the piled-up material would accommodate an extra amount of load but causes 
underestimation of the real contact depth and hence the contact area. On the other hand, the sink-
in effect accounts for the overestimation of the real contact area and hence decrease of the 
calculated mechanical properties. Both of these cases would cause problems in the calculation 
using Oliver-Pharr method, which was first established for monolithic materials use only, as the 
calculated mechanical properties deviate from the true values. Therefore, in the absence of the 
pile-up and sink-in effects, the calculation in this study using Oliver-Pharr method could 
generate more accurate estimation towards the real mechanical properties of the film.  
In nanoindentation tests of thin films, one of the key difficulties is to avoid errors in 
measuring film properties caused by influence of the substrate. To do so, an appropriate 
indentation depth (hmax) and/or an indent load (Pmax) are desired by taking into account the test 
film’s thickness and the nature of the substrate (whether it has higher or lower elastic modulus 
than the film). Indenting to a depth less than 10% of the thickness of a film (namely hmax/tf < 0.1) 
has been empirically considered as a safe condition to avoid effects from substrate and extract 
intrinsic film properties in routine nanoindentation tests16. However, it can be found in our case 
that the effect from substrate was much less significant for indentation depth up to 0.2tf, as 
shown in Figure 3. Moreover, the influence of the substrate was not as marked for films sintered 
at 1100 and 1200 °C, as the plots stabilised as plateaus after indentation depth increased beyond 
0.2tf. This was most probably because the stiffness of these films was closer to that of the 
substrate. 
Figure 6 (a) shows the “plastic” zone (higher density caused by crushing deformation) 
under an indent performed at a relatively deep penetration (hmax/tf = 0.25) in a LSCF film after 
sintering at 1100 °C reaching as far as the interface with the substrate. Measurements of the local 
“density” using the method described earlier are presented in Figure 6 (b), which shows that the 
local density close to the substrate was 79.6%, greater than the density of the material away from 
the indent (74.4%). This indicates that the substrate was interfered with the progression of the 
“plastic” zone and, by inference, the elastic zone beyond. Therefore in this particular case the 
ratio hmax/tf = 0.25 was clearly too large for the results not to be influenced by the substrate and, 
since the substrate possessed a higher modulus (221 GPa) than the film, the measured film 
modulus E = 115 GPa is larger than the true value of approximately 90 GPa. To avoid the 
substrate effect, a lower penetration of around 0.15tf was applied as shown in Figure 7. In this 
case the “plastic” zone had less densification and did not reach the substrate. The modulus of 93 
GPa obtained is therefore significantly smaller.

Further evidence of substrate effect can be observed as below. Figure 8 shows 
representative SEM images of the indentation imprints and their cross-sections at indentation 
depths from 800 to 3600 nm in an 1100 °C-sintered film (tf ࡉ 10 μm) obtained using the 
FIB/SEM instrument. The imprints for indentation depth lower than 800 nm (i.e. hmax/tf < 8%) 
could not be identified with SEM because the residual depths were too shallow. To simplify the 
image acquisition process, no surface protection coating or impregnation was applied to this 
sample.

Figure 8. Top surface and cross-sectional SEM images of indents corresponding to maximum

The results clearly show no delamination of the films from the substrates, indicating good 
interfacial adhesion. Increasing compaction of the film against the substrate can be found at large 
indentation depth. In this film a critical indentation depth of approximately 2000 nm (hmax/tf = 
20%) can be deduced above which the “plastic” deformation region reached the film/substrate 
interface and the substrate had a significant effect on the apparent modulus of the film.  
In the literature, most studies have adopted the approximate guideline of limiting the 
indentation depth to less than one tenth of the film thickness in order to obtain reliable values for 
the elastic modulus of the film16. In the present study we have shown that, for the materials 
investigated here, indentation depths up to 20% of film thickness could be used before the 
influence of the substrate became much prominent. 
It can be concluded from above analysis that indenting shallower than 0.1tf the results 
were unduly influenced by surface roughness and above 0.2tf they were increasingly influenced 
by the substrate. Therefore, the most reliable data points for the films to be extrapolated to 
estimate true properties in this study are those with the ratio hmax/tf in the range 0.1 to 0.2, where 
little influence from surface roughness and substrate were introduced. Consequently, 
extrapolations were carried out to zero hmax/tf by ignoring these most affected data range, as 
shown in Figure 3, particularly for the films sintered at lower temperatures (i.e. 900 and 
1000 °C). After all, it is always preferable to deduce elastic modulus values from a measured 
plateau rather than an overall fit. The estimated results were obtained as listed in Table II.

It is evident to find from the table that the elastic modulus increased dramatically with 
sintering temperature as further densification took place at higher temperatures. For example, the 
elastic modulus rose nearly fourfold across the whole range of sintering temperatures. Figure 9 
further shows the measured elastic modulus as a function of porosity for the as-sintered films.  
 
 
Figure 9. Elastic modulus vs. porosity for as-sintered LSCF films 
 
The solid fit in Figure 9 suggests a fairly exponential relationship between modulus and 
porosity, confirming a major influence of porosity on modulus, although there might be some 
other factors to control the elastic modulus.  
 
3D Reconstruction and Finite Element Modelling 
At least three independent volumes of interest were sectioned for each film to perform the 
3D microstructure reconstruction and the subsequent FEM. The average total time consumed to 
complete the simulation was less than 10 minutes for each microstructure model, on a Dell 
PRECISION T5500 workstation with a Linux OS and an Intel Xeon quad-core 64 bit 2.76 GHz 
processor with 46 GB RAM. Some of the detailed data on the segmentation, meshing, and FEM 
processes are presented in Table III. Note that the varied thresholding values (which was set as 
the value of the valley between the two peaks in the grayscale histogram) for segmentation of 
different image stacks acquired are attributed to their respective grayscale distribution 
(brightness and contrast) caused by the image acquisition conditions of the time. From the table, 
it is also found that with the increasing sintering temperature (i.e. reducing porosity as shown 
earlier), the LSCF voxel number increased while the element number after meshing decreased 
and hence the total simulation time was also diminished. This was because lower porosity could

The reconstructed 3D microstructures are shown in Figure 10 (a) - (d) in the order of 
increasing sintering temperature from 900 to 1200 °C. From these reconstructed microstructures, 
it can be readily observed that the gradual densification process took place with reducing 
porosity due to the increasing sintering temperature.   
 
 
Figure 10. Examples of the reconstructed 3D microstructures of the 900-1200 °C as-sintered 
LSCF films, with voids being pores 
 
Although assumptions of ideal material properties such as linear elasticity and isotropy 
were considered during simulations of the 3D reconstructed microstructures, the isotropy of the 
actual microstructures remained questioned. One of the factors responsible for the fact that the 
microstructures of the films could not possibly be ideally isotropic was that the films experienced 
constrained sintering which induced greater lateral shrinkage/densification across the direction 
normal to the surface than that in the other two directions which might result in non-identical 
X
Y
Z
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
123
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
physical and mechanical properties. Therefore, in our study we intended to understand the 
anisotropy of the films’ microstructures by comparing the possibly directionally dependent 
elastic moduli calculated by FEM based on the reconstructed 3D microstructures.    
Table IV shows the resulting elastic modulus in the three directions for the 
microstructures of films sintered at varying temperatures. Note that as explained earlier, Y 
direction which is normal to the film surface is the direction of nanoindentation. Therefore, E2 
would be used to compare with the elastic modulus resulted from nanoindentation. 
 
Table IV Effective elastic moduli calculated of actual reconstructed 3D models 
Sintering Temperature (°C) E in X (E1) (GPa)

E in Z (E3) (GPa)

111.8±4.6 
 
From the table, it can be seen that for all the samples the elastic modulus in the direction 
Y possessed a relatively larger value compared to that in the other two directions (i.e. X and Z), 
regardless the sintering temperature. This anisotropy is expected from the aforementioned 
constrained sintering during which grain boundary diffusion and surface diffusion along Y 
direction are inactivated due to the tensile stress in the film plane, while diffusion parallel to the 
film plane is active because there is no constraint in Y direction. As a result, atoms of the solid 
phase are diffused to fill the pore area parallel to the film plane, resulting in the contraction of 
the pores along X-Z directions and thus elongation normal to the film plane, although these 
differences can’t be readily distinguished in the 2D SEM image shown in the reconstructed 3D 
microstructures shown in Figure 10. The resulted necks of the overlapped grains perpendicular to 
the film plane are relatively bigger than those with other orientations, particularly in the initial 
stages of sintering or after sintering at lower temperatures. This is consistent with the observation 
found by Wang37 in the constrained sintering of YSZ films: the pores are preferentially oriented 
in the direction of normal to the film plane. Hence the modulus in direction Y is larger. 
Therefore, it would be very useful to determine the neck size and neck orientation as well as their 
effect on the mechanical properties of the films.  
In order to effectively assess the variation of the directionally dependent elastic modulus, 
the results were normalised relative to elastic modulus along Y direction, i.e. E* = Ei/E2 (i=1, 2 
and 3), as shown in Figure 11. The comparison and difference can be more readily seen with 
normalised average values added. The degree of anisotropy (DA) is thus be calculated and 
plotted for each sintering temperature: DA=1-smallest normalised modulus, with DA=0 when 
fully isotropic and DA=1, fully anisotropic. 
 
124
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Determination of Elastic Moduli for Porous SOFC Cathode Films
 
Figure 11. Normalised elastic modulus in three directions for the as-sintered films  
 
In Figure 11, it is evident that the relative error of the normalised average became 
increasingly smaller as the sintering temperature rose. Meanwhile, the elastic moduli in X and Z 
directions experienced similar increase and thus tended to gradually approach the modulus in Y 
direction with the rising sintering degree. Consequently, the degree of anisotropy decreases from 
about 0.4 to 0.1 with the increasing temperature of sintering from 900-1200 °C, attributable to a 
number of inter-related factors: (i) the elimination of pores by densification during sintering, (ii) 
the slowed grain boundary diffusion rate due to the longer grain boundary diffusion path 
generated by the increasing neck size, and (iii) the reduction of difference in neck size in 
different orientations due to surface diffusion.  
The comparison of nanoindentation result with the elastic modulus data derived from 
FEM is shown in Figure 12. In the figure, the elastic modulus variation was calculated by

Figure 12. Comparison of elastic moduli measured by nanoindentation and calculated by FEM 
 
As explained earlier, the values for X and Z directions in the film plane are very close to 
each other but smaller than that for Y (vertical direction), which were due to the constrained 
sintering mechanism. Although the samples exhibited varying degrees of anisotropy, the average 
values were considered to better represent the true elastic modulus values of the porous thin 
films. The decreasing elastic modulus variation with the increasing sintering temperature also 
shows diminishing sensitivity of the films’ isotropy on sintering degree. Furthermore, the figure 
indicates that the elastic modulus measured by nanoindentation for each sintering temperature 
was reasonably consistent with the average of the elastic moduli in the three directions within 
experimental errors, although slightly lower values derived from FEM than that from 
nanoindentation were found for films sintered at 1000 and 1100 °C sintered films. The 
experimental errors of the elastic moduli calculated by FEM are considered to be accounted by 
the porosity variation in different sampling locations. 
One may argue that the statistics was insufficiently convincible, but since the agreement 
was for all cases of the samples after sintering at varying four temperatures the results are 
reasonably representative even though the number of sampling locations per sample was 
relatively small. After all, the FEM calculation based on 3D reconstruction could be an effective 
means to evaluate the mechanical properties of the porous films, e.g. elastic modulus of the 
LSCF cathode in the current study.    
 
CONCLUSION 
Elastic moduli of porous partially sintered LSCF cathode films were measured using 
nanoindentation experiments and analysis. The 3D microstructures of the films was studied by 
3D reconstruction of serial sections obtained using a dual-beam FIB/SEM technique. Porosity 
and effective elastic modulus were calculated respectively using 3D quantification package and 
the finite element modelling based on the reconstructed microstructures. The experiments 
showed that reliable measurements of the true film properties were obtained by data 
extrapolation provided that indentation depth was 0.1-0.2 of the film thickness. Effects from 
surface roughness and substrates responsible for the data deviation were taken into account. It 
was found that the elastic modulus increased from 32-122GPa as the sintering temperature raised

from 900–1200°C and the porosity decreased from 47-15%. Despite the existence of certain

in good agreement to the experimental results measured by nanoindentation. The present method 
could be an effective tool for investigating the way in which the mechanical performance of 
electrodes is influenced by their microstructures.

MECHANICAL MODELING OF MICROCRACKED POROUS CERAMICS 
 
Ray S. Fertig IIIa and Seth Nickersonb 
aDept. of Mechanical Engineering, University of Wyoming, Laramie, WY, USA 
bCorning Incorporated, Corning, NY, USA & Bauhaus University, Weimar, Germany 
 
ABSTRACT 
The focus of this work is to develop an approach for predicting microcracking in porous 
ceramics with anisotropic crystalline thermal properties, such as cordierite, arising from 
combined thermal and mechanical loading. A previous model for predicting thermally-induced 
microcracking based on the distribution of grain boundary tractions is expanded upon to predict 
the increase in grain boundary microcracks with increasing applied tensile load. A method for 
linking isotropic distributions of thermally-induced microcracks with uniaxial tensile load-
induced anisotropic distributions of microcracks is proposed. The developed model contains only 
a single additional parameter beyond the thermal model, which characterizes load transfer from 
the macroscopic structure to grain boundary tractions.  The model is used to predict microcrack-
controlled nonlinear constitutive tensile behavior of a microcracked porous ceramic at a wide 
range of temperatures. 
 
INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND 
Porous ceramics used in environmental control technologies such as catalytic converters 
and diesel particulate filters are often subjected to very large changes in operating temperature 
and high thermal gradients. Typically refractory ceramics, such as cordierite, are used in these 
applications to mitigate the effects of thermal shock. One feature of these materials is that their 
microstructure changes continuously through the formation and healing of microcracks 
throughout the thermomechanical loading process. The effects of these microcracks on the 
constitutive behavior of the ceramics are complicated; not only is the density of cracks 
important, but the effect of friction on existing cracks can play a significant role in the 
constitutive response. The focus of the work reported here is to develop a model with parameters 
corresponding to meaningful microstructure features, which enable predicting microcrack 
evolution under combined thermal and mechanical loading. This microcrack information is then 
used to predict the constitutive response of a porous ceramic to tensile loading. 
 
MODEL DEVELOPMENT 
The fundamental hypothesis for the model is that grain boundary quantities such as 
traction, traction ratio, and crack opening displacement can be represented as statistical 
distributions. Simple relationships can then be developed between these distributions and 
macroscopic quantities such as stress and temperature. The result of this approach will be a 
multiscale model with physically meaningful parameters. Although the microstructure of the 
porous ceramics under consideration is very complex, the features of the microstructure 
contribute to a statistical distribution of the microstructure if a sufficient quantity of material is 
considered. For example, the starting point for the model development is the distribution of grain 
boundary tractions in uncracked boundaries, as shown in Figure 1. Under changing temperature, 
this distribution spreads out—its standard deviation increases and the mean remains at zero net 
stress (static equilibrium) 1. Under applied tensile load this distribution shifts to represent an 
externally applied load. The distribution contains a single critical, threshold microstructural 
feature—the grain boundary strength. The constructed model only requires the relationship 
between macroscopic temperature or stress to the spreading or the shifting, respectively, to 
complete a multiscale model that predicts the effects of temperature or stress on cracking.

Tensile Loading-Induced Microcracking 
Equation (7) describes the evolution of normalized isotropic microcrack density during

was assumed to occur. However, during tensile loading the additional cracking will be

cracks are related using Equation (4).

Effect of Friction 
Frictional sliding becomes an important phenomenon when cracks close and likely 
contributes to the macroscopic constitutive behavior of the porous material. For the sake of 
simplicity, a Coulomb friction law is assumed such that slip occurs when

RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
To test the results of the model developed here, results are compared with room 
temperature tensile loading data for a porous cordierite. The modulus-temperature behavior for 
this ceramic is shown in Figure 2. From this data, values for  and  were computed. The 
coefficient of friction for this material is not known, but reported values for ceramics range from 
0.6 - 1.8 4-6. For this work, a nominal value of  = 1.0 was selected. The other needed parameters 
of interest are given in Table 1 along with the method used for determining their values.

Mechanical Modeling of Microcracked Porous Ceramics
Using the parameters given in Table 1, the model was used to compute the tensile 
loading-unloading curve for a sample loaded to 4 MPa. Figure 5 shows the predicted response 
along with the experimental loading data. Three features of these results are notable. First, 
excellent agreement is observed on loading. Second, a reasonable loading-unloading hysteresis 
was predicted. (Because unloading data for this sample was not available, validation of 
unloading was unable to be made.) Finally, the only “fitted” parameter to this data was . The 
other parameters were either measured directly or given reasonable physical values. 
 
 
Figure 5. Constitutive behavior under tensile loading and unloading. 
 
SUMMARY AND CONCLUSION 
In this work, a model was developed for predicting microcrack density evolution in 
porous ceramics in response to combined thermal and mechanical loading. The microcrack 
density was used to predict effective elastic modulus and constitutive response of the 
microcracked material. The driving paradigm for this model was the representation of grain 
boundary quantities such as traction, traction ratio, and crack opening displacement as statistical 
distributions. Simple relationship were developed between these distributions and macroscopic 
quantities such as stress and temperature. This enabled a multiscale model to be developed with 
physically meaningful parameters. Our preliminary results showed good agreement with 
experiment. Further studies are underway to validate this model.

SYNTHESIS AND CHARACTERIZATION OF AEROGEL GLASS MATERIALS FOR 
WINDOW GLAZING APPLICATIONS 
 
Tao Gaoa, Bjørn Petter Jelleb,c, Arild Gustavsena, and Jianying Hed 
a Department of Architectural Design, History and Technology, Norwegian University of 
Science and Technology (NTNU), NO-7491 Trondheim, Norway. 
b Department of Materials and Structures, SINTEF Building and Infrastructure, NO-7465 
Trondheim, Norway.  
c Department of Civil and Transport Engineering, Norwegian University of Science and 
Technology (NTNU), NO-7491 Trondheim, Norway. 
d Department of Structural Engineering, Norwegian University of Science and Technology 
(NTNU), NO-7491 Trondheim, Norway.  
 
ABSTRACT  
Sol-gel derived aerogel glass materials have been prepared by sintering an acid-catalyzed 
silica aerogel precursor. The physical and chemical processes during the sol 
 gel 
 glass 
transformation have been discussed. The obtained aerogel glass materials are characterized by 
low mass density (   1.8 g/cm3), low thermal conductivity (k  0.17 W/(mK)), high visible 
transparency (Tvis  95.4% at 500 nm), and improved mechanical strength (reduced modulus Er  
6.42 GPa). Strategies that enable to enhance further the mechanical strength and minimize the 
manufacturing costs of aerogel glass materials are also described.  
 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Silica aerogels are an extremely porous (porosity 80–99.8%), low-density (~0.003–
0.5 g/cm3), and low-thermal conductivity (~0.005–0.1 W/(mK)) solid material with many 
important applications.1 In building and construction sector, for example, silica aerogels have 
already been used as a promising thermal insulation material.2 Silica aerogels have also found 
applications as lightweight aggregates for concrete and cement-based materials.3,4 The fact that 
silica aerogels can be made in large pieces (i.e. monolithic panes) while maintaining a 
transparent state makes silica aerogels an attractive glazing material for superinsulating 
windows.5,6 In fact, the great potential of silica aerogels in the building and construction sector 
have resulted in renewed interest in the production and optimization of silica aerogels with 
improved performance and reduced manufacturing costs.1  
We are interested in high performance windows that are known to be critical to improve the 
energy efficiency of buildings.7-9 As an interesting window innovation, highly insulating aerogel 
glazing units with U-values (U-value is an overall heat transfer co-efficient and is frequently 
used in building and construction sector. A low U-value indicates a high level of thermal 
insulation) of about 0.5–0.7 W/(m2K) have been reported in the literature.5,6,8 An aerogel glazing 
unit is architecturally similar to the standard double glazing, where the air gap between two glass 
panes is filled with silica aerogels, usually in form of granules.10 It is worth noting that, however, 
the loss of visible transparency remains an obvious drawback for aerogel granule glazings. Better 
performance with respect to high visible transparency is usually achieved with monolithic 
aerogel panes.5,6,10 However, monolithic aerogel panes are very brittle, i.e., having typically a 
flexural strength varying between 1 kPa and 1 MPa,11 which makes their application as window 
glazing materials questionable, e.g. the risk of failure under tensile/bending stresses due to wind 
load and/or external mechanical forces. Improving the mechanical strength of silica aerogels, 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
142
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Synthesis and Characterization of Aerogel Glass Materials for Window Glazing
 
 
especially for the monolithic panes, has without doubts been an important research topic in the 
field.12  
Enhanced mechanical performance of silica aerogels can be achieved via different structural 
reinforcements, for example, by using flexible silica precursors in silica gel backbone,13 
conformal coating of silica backbone via surface cross-linking with polymers,14,15 and dispersing 
reinforced fibers in the initial sol of silica aerogels.16 It is worth noting that many of these 
mechanical reinforcing strategies fail to produce strong silica aerogels with a transparent state, 
which is, however, a critical requirement for their potential application as glazing materials. 
We report in this work an approach to enhance the mechanical strength of silica aerogels via 
densification. Although the loss of porosity and consequently the increase of thermal 
conductivity of silica aerogels represent drawbacks related to the densification process,17,18 a 
combination of enhanced mechanical performance and optical transparency indicates that the 
densificated silica aerogels may be used as new glass material for window glazing application. 
Preliminary experimental results indicate lightweight (density  
 1.8 g/cm3, compared to 
2.5 g/cm3 for float glass) and thermal insulating (thermal conductivity k 
 0.18 W/(mK), 
compared to about 0.92 W/(mK) for float glass) aerogel glass materials with high visible 
transparency (Tvis  95.4% at 500 nm, compared to 92.0% for float glass) can be achieved by 
annealing an acid-catalyzed silica aerogel precursor at 700°C. Typical elastic modulus Er of the 
obtained aerogel glass materials is about 6.42 GPa, which can be further enhanced by, e.g. 
increasing the annealing temperatures.18  
 
EXPERIMENTAL 
 
Materials and Chemicals 
Reagent-grade tetraethyl orthosilicate (TEOS), ethanol (EtOH, 96%), nitric acid (HNO3, 
68%), sulfuric acid (H2SO4, 98%), and hydrogen peroxide (H2O2, 30%) were purchased from 
Sigma-Aldrich and used without further purification. Float glass substrates (25 mm × 25 mm × 
1 mm) were cleaned with a Piranha solution (98% H2SO4/30% H2O2, 7/3 in volume ratio), 
washed with distilled water, and dried naturally in air for further use. Distilled water was used 
throughout the experiments. 
 
Synthesis of Aerogel Glass Materials 
For a typical synthesis, TEOS, EtOH and H2O were mixed with a molar ratio of 
TEOS:EtOH:H2O=1:4:24. The hydrolysis of TEOS was catalyzed with the addition of HNO3; 
the final pH value of the obtained mixture was about 2. The hydrolysis reaction was continued 
for 30 min at room temperature under vigorous stirring. Afterwards, the obtained TEOS-EtOH-
H2O-HNO3 solution was transferred into closed flasks and aged in a preheated oven at 60°C for 
1 day to reach a complete gelation. The obtained silica hydrogels were then aged 60°C for 1 
week to improve the strength. Thereafter the temperature of the oven was slowly increased to 
120°C to dry the hydrogels, during which the flasks were partly opened for evaporation purpose. 
The obtained dry gels were then annealed at 700°C in air for 3 hours to give the as-prepared 
aerogel glass materials. 
 
Characterization 
Scanning electron microscopy (SEM, Hitachi S-5500) was employed to characterize the 
morphology and microstructure of the obtained samples.  
Thermogravimetric analysis (TGA) was performed on a Netzsch STA 449C thermo-
microbalance in air at a heating rate of 10°C min-1.  
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
143
Synthesis and Characterization of Aerogel Glass Materials for Window Glazing
 
 
Optical properties of the as-prepared samples were measured on a Perkin Elmer Lambda 
1050 UV/VIS/NIR spectrophotometer. Transmittance spectra were collected from 250 to 
3300 nm with a spectral resolution of 1 nm. Total reflectance spectra were collected with a 
150 mm Integrating Sphere Reflectance Accessory; the samples were measured from 250 to 
2500 nm with a spectral resolution of 1 nm.  
Attenuated total reflectance (ATR) Fourier transform infrared (FTIR) spectra were recorded 
on a Nicolet 8700 FTIR Spectrometer (Thermo Scientific) with a spectral resolution of 2 cm-1.  
Thermal conductivity of the obtained aerogel glass materials was analyzed by using a Hot 
Disk Thermal Constants Analyzer (Model TPS 2500S). A disk-type Kapton Sensor 5465 with 
radius 3.189 mm was used. The temperature increase of the samples as a function of time was 
recorded to compute the thermal conductivity. The final thermal conductivity value reported here 
was the arithmetic mean of three individual measurements under different measurement 
conditions (heating power: 5–20 mW, measurement time: 5–40 s).  
Mechanical measurement of the samples was performed on a Nanomechanical Testing 
System (TriboIndenter® 900, Hysitron, MN), which is capable of both load and displacement 
controlled modes with load and displacement noise floor of 100 nN and 1 nm, respectively. The 
force-displacement curves were recorded to evaluate the corresponding mechanical properties.  
 
RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
 
Sol 
 Gel 
 Glass Transformation 
In silica gels made by the hydrolysis of TEOS in alcoholic solution, the net reaction to form 
silica is generally represented as: 
 
Si(OCH2CH3)4 + 2H2O 
 SiO2 + 4CH3CH2OH.                  (1) 
 
The structural characteristics of the obtained silica materials (i.e. texture, density, etc.) are 
strongly dependent on several experimental parameters, such as TEOS concentration, EtOH:H2O 
ratio, and catalyst.19,20 It has been reported that, during the synthesis of silica aerogels, the initial 
polysilicate species formed from the hydrolysis of TEOS are not dense colloidal particles of 
anhydrous silica but instead solvated polymeric chains or clusters;19 therefore the “sol 
 gel” 
transformation or gelation is actually a densification process where the polymeric chains of 
silicate species are jointed together through desiccation. Since silica is mostly active in basic 
environment, it is not surprising that the pH value of the reaction system would significantly 
affect the resulted cross-linked silica network. For example, the bulk density of silica aerogels 
can be as low as 0.015 g/cm3 if the sample is prepared under basic conditions and higher than 0.5 
g/cm3 in acidic catalysis.21,22 It indicates that, although silica colloidal particles are not formed 
during the “sol 
 gel” transformation, base catalyzed polysilicate species before gelation are 
highly branched or collapsed, thus behave as discrete clusters; whereas acid catalyzed silicate 
polymers are highly interpenetrated or entangled, resulting in less free volume upon gelation. For 
the synthesis of aerogel glass materials through densification, a higher density is preferred for 
silica aerogel precursors for the subsequent gel 
 glass transformation, which explains the acid 
catalyzed synthetic procedure employed in this work.   
Figure 1 shows the TGA data of the as-prepared silica hydrogels, indicating the presence of 
two distinctive steps, namely dehydration and dehydroxylation, during the “gel 
 glass” 
transformation. A significant weight loss of about 70% at low temperature region (i.e. T < 
130°C) has been observed, which can be attributed to the loss of pore liquid (e.g. H2O and 
EtOH). As the temperature increases, e.g. from 200°C to 1200°C, there is a negligible weight 
144
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Synthesis and Characterization of Aerogel Glass Materials for Window Glazing
 
 
loss of about 2%, which is related to the dehydroxylation of silica. According to the TGA data, a 
dramatic structural evolution at the low temperature region (i.e. T < 130°C) can be expected. In 
fact, the properties of the final product, i.e. aerogel glass materials in this work, are mostly 
determined by a series of successive gelation and aging steps at this low temperature region.  
 
 
Figure 1. TGA curve of the as-prepared silica hydrogels. 
 
Figure 2 shows the FTIR spectra of the silica hydrogels during the drying process. The silica 
hydrogels feature two predominant absorptions at 3300 (actually a broad band ranging from ~ 
3350 to ~ 3250 cm-1) and 1635 cm-1, which are related to stretching and bending vibrations of 
water molecules, respectively.23-26 The stretching and bending vibrations of the Si-O-Si bridges 
are observed at about 1082 and 437 cm-1, respectively.24-26 The weak absorption at about 970 cm-
1 can be attributed to the surface Si-OH groups.23 Leaving the sample in air for a few hours (e.g. 
3 h in this work) results in the loss of a certain amount of pore liquid, which can readily be 
observed by FTIR spectra, Figure 2b. For example, the intensity of the band at 3300 and 
1635 cm-1 has been drastically attenuated. There are also changes on the band positions and 
intensities for the Si-O and Si-OH vibrations. The fact that these vibrations usually shift to the 
lower wavenumber side (e.g., 1082 
 1032, 970 
 940, 437 
 408 cm-1) indicates a 
continuous gelation and aging process,17-19 where the siloxane bonds are strengthened.25

Figure 2. FTIR spectra of the original silica hydrogels (a) and those aged in air for 3 h at 
room temperature (b). 
 
Aerogel Glass Materials 
According to the TGA data (Figure 1), an annealing treatment performed at 700°C has been 
used to prepare aerogel glass materials in this work. The annealing treatment has also been 
performed with different temperatures, where the data will be interpreted and reported later.  
Figure 3 shows typical SEM images of the as-prepared aerogel glass. In general, transparent 
and clear glass-like materials are always obtained in this work. The as-prepared aerogel glass 
materials are flat and smooth (Figure 3a). Detailed SEM analysis (Figure 3b) shows that the as-
prepared aerogel glass materials are actually aggregations of fine particles (diameter < 50 nm), 
which are similar to those observed for silica aerogels.1 
 
 
 
Figure 3. SEM images of the as-prepared aerogel glass materials.  
 
As a potential glazing material, optical properties of the as-prepared aerogel glass materials 
are obviously important. Figure 4 shows the measured UV-VIS-NIR transmittance spectra of the 
as-prepared aerogel glass materials. It can be seen that the materials have a higher visible 
transmittance, Tvis, of about 95.4% at 500 nm, compared to that of the float glass, 92.0%. The 
increased visible transparency of aerogel glass materials represents an interesting feature for 
their applications as glazing materials. Moreover, the obtained aerogel glass materials are 
146
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Synthesis and Characterization of Aerogel Glass Materials for Window Glazing
 
 
probably IR-absorbing, e.g. in the wavelength region of 2500 – 3300 nm, which may be relevant 
for the heat management through the window aperture.8  
 
 
Figure 4. UV-VIS-IR transmittance spectra of the as-prepared aerogel glass and float glass 
for comparison. The absorptions at 860 and 1800 nm are related to detector changes.   
 
Thermal conductivities of the as-synthesized aerogel glass materials have been measured by 
using a transient plane source technique.27 Figure 5 reports the transient graph of the aerogel 
glass, together with that of gloat glass for comparison. The measurement is based on the time-
dependent temperature increase T( ) given by:27

where P0 is the total output of power from the sensor,  is the overall radius of the disk spiral (  
= 3.189 mm in this work), k is the thermal conductivity of the sample, and D( ) is a 
dimensionless time-dependent function. Apparently, a computational plot of T( ) vs. D( ) will 
result in a straight line, of which the slope is correlated to the heating power P0, the radius of the 
disk spiral , and the thermal conductivity k. Hence, the thermal conductivity of the samples can 
be calculated. In this work, the as-prepared aerogel glass materials have a thermal conductivity 
of about 0.17 W/(mK), compared to about 0.92 W/(mK) for float glass.

The mechanical performance of the obtained aerogel glass materials has been evaluated by 
using the nanoindentation technique. Figure 6 shows the force–displacement curves during the 
measurement, from which the hardness H and elastic modulus Er of the samples can be 
calculated:28 
A

max                                 (4)

where Pmax is the maximum load, A is the contact area between the indenter and the material 
surface,  is a constant related to the shape of the indenter, and S is the slope of the initial part of 
the unloading curve. A detailed theoretical consideration has been reported by Fischer-Cripps.29 
In this work, the as-prepared aerogel glass materials exhibit typically a hardness of about 0.53 
GPa and an elastic modulus of about 6.42 GPa. Although the as-prepared aerogel glass materials 
show a pronounced enhancement in mechanical performance of up to three orders of magnitude 
when compared to silica aerogel (i.e. typical elastic modulus of silica aerogels  1–10 MPa),30 
they are probably still weak for glazing applications. For example, float glass has a hardness of 
about 1.64 GPa and an elastic modulus of about 50.77 GPa. In this regard, further work is 
necessary to enhance the mechanical performance of aerogel glass materials while at the same 
time maintaining their improved optical and thermal insulation performance.

Table 1 summarizes the properties of the as-prepared aerogel glass materials in this work. It 
is obviously necessary and important to enhance further the mechanical properties of aerogel 
glass materials for window glazing applications. Increasing the annealing temperature seems to 
be a straightforward method,17,18,20 though the increased energy consumption and the risk of 
increased thermal conductivity need to be considered. From an economical and large scale 
production point of view, there are also other issues that may affect the process economics. For 
example, the as-prepared aerogel glass materials in this work are pure silica, which may have a 
high material cost when compared to that of float glass. Substituting partly silica with other 
materials such as lime (i.e. CaO) may be interesting to reduce the global cost of the final 
products, though the newly introduced physical and chemical processes during the “sol 
 gel 
 
glass” transformation would require further research efforts.

CONCLUSIONS  
Lightweight and thermal insulating aerogel glass materials with excellent visible 
transparency can be prepared by sintering silica aerogel precursors at high temperatures. During 
the densification process, aerogel glass materials gain also enhanced mechanical strength. These 
structural and functional features make aerogel glass materials interesting and attractive for 
window glazing applications, where improving the thermal insulation and reducing the weight of 
the glazing units are important for high performance windows. Further studies are still necessary 
to understand the detailed physical and/or chemical processes during the synthesis of aerogel 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
149
Synthesis and Characterization of Aerogel Glass Materials for Window Glazing
 
 
glass materials in order to improve further the material performance and to reduce the 
manufacture cost for large scale productions.

RETICULATED CERAMICS UNDER BENDING: THE NON-LINEAR REGIME BEFORE 
THEIR CATASTROPHIC FAILURE 
Ehsan Rezaei, Giovanni Bianchi,  Alberto Ortona,
ICIMSI-SUPSI, Strada Cantonale Galleria 2 , 6928, Manno, Switzerland. 
Sandro Gianella
EngiCer, Viale Pereda 22, 6828, Balerna, Switzerland. 
 
ASBTRACT 
Our early experiences with reticulated ceramics under mechanical loads were always 
showing a behavior which is not typical for monolithic ceramics whichare characterized by a 
sudden and catastrophic failure. Conversely, as the material takes the morphology of a solid 
foam, before collapsing, local failure of its struts and nodes is occurring. This phenomenon 
motivated us to further study this non-linear regime in their mechanical behavior. In this work, Si 
infiltrated SiC foams and lattices were mechanically tested by four point bending. In order to 
better understand this phenomenon we coupled standard sensing devices (a linear potentiometric 
position sensor and a load cell) with electrical resistance and acoustical emissions sensors. The 
results show a strong correlation between local failures, electrical resistance and acoustical 
output. We observed that, unlike monoliths, reticulated ceramics experience local cracks in 
multiple zones at the same time. As a result of this, ceramic foams present a certain “degree of 
toughness” which could not be possible if they were monolithic. We show also that the quantity 
of molten Si employed in liquid silicon infiltration, (filling first the intra particle porosity and 
then, if in excess, the cavity of the struts) has a great influence on the final strength of the foam. 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Reticulated porous ceramics are a group of cellular ceramics containing cells internally 
attached to each other. Due to the high temperature stability of their skeleton material (above 
1000°C) and  the low density they can be found in advanced engineering applications like 
filtration, components in solid oxide fuel cells, light weight sandwich structures, fixtures for 
space mirrors and solar absorbers. [1][2] [3][4]. There are different techniques for manufacturing 
ceramic foams. Replication of polymeric template foams, direct foaming, and the use of fugitive 
pore formers are considered as the main methods.[5] The material used in this work is a so called 
Si-SiC reticulated foam. It is produced by replica technique as the most well-known method of 
manufacturing ceramic foams.[5][1] Silicon infiltration is then used to convert the residual 
carbons in the pyrolyzed foam into SiC. 
Monolithic ceramics show a linear behavior during mechanical tests and they collapse by 
a sudden propagation of micro-cracks which results in a so called “snap-back” or  catastrophic 
failure.[6] However, the behavior of ceramic foams under bending is rather different from a 
brittle failure. They have a non-linear behavior which allows them to be more damageable.[7][8] 
Meille et. al.[8] observed a relationship between the porosity of the ceramics and their 
damageability. Other studies have tried to understand the influence of pore morphology and pore 
spacing in progressive damage of the porous materials.[9][10][11] This work investigates the 
behavior of ceramic foams in their nonlinear regime discussing the influence of macroporosities 
and microporosities in their behavior. To do this, a number of samples have been tested under 
flexural loading.  
On the other side, integrity monitoring techniques must be used to get more information 
on the state of the damage in the structure. Piezoelectric sensors and optical fibers have been 
used for health monitoring of polymer composites, however their use is rather expensive, 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
152
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Reticulated Ceramics under Bending: Non-Linear Regime before Catastrophic Failure
complicated and maybe even unpractical for the ceramic foams. Besides, techniques such as X-
ray, ultrasonic C-scan and infrared thermography normally need the piece to be disassembled 
from its application to go under the testing machine. Recently some researchers have tried to use 
electrical resistance of the material as a self sensing technique of the damage in the 
material.[12][13][14] This method can be industrially practical as it is cheap, simple, doesn’t 
need any embedded sensor within the material and can give real time data about the state of 
damage in the structure. Furthermore, despite the embedded or attached sensors that tend to 
allow for local monitoring, it can monitor the entire structure.[12] Obviously, the use of this 
method depends on the conductivity of the material. Sensitivity of the electrical resistance of the 
component to different cracks also plays an important role in effectiveness of this method. [15] 
The functionality of electrical resistance(ER) monitoring and a low frequency acoustical 
emission(ACE) to detect damage in ceramic foams will be discussed in this work. 
 
EXPERIMENTAL DETAILS 
The specimens are Si-SiC open-cell foams produced by Erbicol SA, Switzerland. Replica 
technique and Si infiltration have been used which consist of three steps: 
polyurethane foams (10 PPI)  are impregnated into a slurry made of ͣ-SiC, binder and 
solvent.  
After drying, the material is pyrolized to form a carbonaceous macro porous foam. 
Si lumps are then placed on the carbonaceous foam and it is infiltrated by molten Si in 
temperatures ranging 1450°C-1600°C to form ͤ-SiC through reaction bonding with 
carbon, based on the following chemical reaction[13]: 
Siliquid + Csolid = SiCsolid   [͇H = -73kj/mol] 
The product is a macro porous open cell foam with a skeletons consisting of ͤ-SiC bound 
with the initial ͣ-SiC powders and Si. [16] Three batches of Si-SiC foams with different Si 
content were made. The carbonaceous foam for all the three types are the same and just the 
amount of Si used for infiltration changes. Test pieces are cut into bars of 
(15 25 170)mm3±0.05. The lower and upper surfaces which are in contact with the bending 
fixtures are machined in order to satisfy the flatness and parallelism conditions for a bending test. 
Copper plates and thin copper wires were used for the brazing and ER measurements. 
Brazing was performed using Cusil-ABA paste from Morgan Crucible Company, UK. The 
process of ER measurement is done all by Labview using National Instruments’ DAQ9219. 
Acoustical variations were recorded by a microphone(Sennheiser MD321N). The data were 
recorded in the frequency of 51.2kHz  by DAQ 9234 from National Instruments. The four point 
flexure tests were performed using a universal testing machine(Zwick/Roell Z050). A 5kN load 
cell and a linear potentiometric position sensor(LPPS) were used to measure the vertical load and 
the deflection of the beam. The crosshead speed of 0.1mm/min and preload of 10N was chosen 
for all the samples. All information were recorded and synchronized using National 
Instruments’s DAQ 9219 and Labview. An isolating box was built to separate the testing 
environment in order to prepare an stabilized temperature inside the box and also to reduce the 
ambient noise. Bend testing loading points were also all isolated from the test pieces by very thin 
plastic sheets.

Comparing the three batches together shows that as Si content increases the mechanical 
properties of the foams are enhanced. This was expected because when Si is introduced to the 
carbonaceous foam, it starts to form ͤ-SiC and also fill the microporosities inside the struts 
which improves the mechanical properties of the skeleton. After filling the porosity, the extra 
amount of Si starts to fill the triangular cavity inside all the struts. This behavior results in a 
higher density for the foams with higher Si content. This is different than literature works that 
typically increase the relative density by changing the struts’ thickness. A schematic image of a 
cross section of struts with different content of free Si is depicted in Figure 1 comparing the 
struts in the current study with literature. 
 
 
Figure 1. A schematic image comparing the two approach for increasing SiSiC foams’ relative 
density. (A)Increasing relative density by decreasing macroporosities in the skeleton. 
(B)Increasing the relative density by increasing the struts’ diameter. The black parts are the 
porosities inside the struts, the white ones are the filled porosities by Si and gray color shows 
SiC.

Bending 
Figure 5 shows the nonlinear saw-tooth shape behavior observed for three samples of each 
batch of our foams. All the foams were broken in two parts with behaviors similar to what is 
shown in Figure 5. Mechanical behavior of these foams under bending  faces two main regimes: 
A linear elastic regime due to the deflection of struts which is the same as a brittle 
behavior before failure. 
A nonlinear regime which continues by failing of more struts until the abrupt final failure 
occurs.

The low Weibull modulus for batch C and also B reveals high amount of scatter in the 
results and a wide distribution of flaws in the sample. The reason can be due to presence of the 
triangular cavity inside the ligaments which are suspected to be the main origin of failure in the 
struts.[18] These cavities are mainly filled with Si for samples of group A which have a higher 
Weibull modulus and also a much higher flexural strength. 
Images of the sample pieces were taken before and after the test. Figure 7 displays failure 
plane location in each of the samples. In batch A, all the samples collapsed between the upper 
two loading points. In batch B one specimen and in batch C almost three specimen failed out of 
the pure bending area. This can be due to the reason that for weaker samples the uniformity of 
the load becomes more and more important in flexural tests. On the other side, for materials with 
low Weibull modulus it is normal to have a number of samples(around 10% of them) fracturing 
outside of the inner gage area. 
 
 
Figure 7. Frequency of failure location. From left to right for batch A, B and C. 
 
To understand better about the location of the cracks, one of the samples (from batch A) 
was chosen to be stopped in the middle of bending test and was observed under the microscope. 
Microscopic observations showed that all the observable cracks were on the tension side of the 
specimen (and not the compression side) which means that the tension side of the foam fails 
faster than the compression side.(Figure 8)  
 
 
Figure 8. Cracks observed in middle of the bending test. 
 
Damage monitoring 
It was interesting to see that both ER and ACE techniques show good correlations with 
the measured results of the vertical load over the samples. Noises in the frequencies out of 2-
20kHz were cut by means of a butterfly filter. ACE and ER data had the noise amplitude of 
0.21±0.3dB and 1±0.5mࠫ respectively. At last, a threshold of 1dB and 3mࠫ were defined as 
minimum ACE and ER variations. The hits(events) were then saved into MATLAB objects 
whenever a change higher than noise levels was observed in each of load, ACE amplitudes or ER 
values. Figure 9 and 10 show an example of data captured by acquisition systems.

Figure 14. Histogram of the Amplitude range for each batch. 
 
A probability distribution of the amplitude range for ACE data(Figure 14) shows that the 
probability that a failed strut or node emits a low ACE energy is higher for batch C while batch 
A and B released sounds in a wider range of amplitudes.  As depicted in Figure 15 and Figure 16 
both ER and ACE show well correlations with the load drops in each event. Based on these 
information, it can be hypothesized that the high amplitude events attributed to the big nodes or 
fully filled struts while the low amplitude ones are related to very weak struts that are highly 
porous or contain pre-existing cracks. As it is mentioned also by Dharmasena et al.[19] in ER 
monitoring the current passes mainly through the struts parallel to the main current flow. Thus 
events with high ACE amplitude and low ER might be due to the failure of ligaments 
perpendicular to current flow through the cells.

circles represent the A, B and C batches. each circle is an event recorded by ER measurement 
system.

CONCLUSION 
Three batches of Si-SiC replicated foams with the same morphologies (pore size and 
elongation) and different Si content were examined under four point flexural test. The use of ER 
and ACE monitoring devices along with LPPS and the load cell resulted in a set of data giving 
in-situ information about the introduced damage in the foams. ACE technique showed to be 
more reliable in detecting the cracks. Results of ER monitoring were in accordance with the 
other data but practical use of this method needs further investigation to improve the sensibility 
of the technique. By increasing Si content in the foam, firstly ͤ-SiC is formed, and then the 
micro-porosities and the cavities inside the ligaments are filled with Si which results from a 
stronger constituent material and hence a stronger foam. Microscopic pictures were also 
confirming the fact that the micro and macro porosities are filled gradually by increasing the Si 
content. Weibull moduli of 7.58, 4.30 and 3.66 for batch A, B and C respectively, showed the 
high scatter in the strength results, especially for batch C. It is presumed that microporosities 
inside the struts of batch B and C cause a wide flaw distribution resulting in low Weibull moduli. 
On the other side the results of monitoring devices showed that the hits/sample parameter varies 
similar to the Weibull moduli(7.8, 5.5 and 4.6 for batch A, B and C respectively). A brittle 
material fails catastrophically and will have a hits/sample ratio equal to one. As the hits/sample 
ratio increases the material shows a higher toughness and will fail due to accumulation of cracks 
in the failure zone. Higher values for batch A shows that the behavior of the foam is more 
successful in stopping the rapid accumulation of cracks in the failure zone.

NOVEL LOW TEMPERATURE CERAMICS FOR CO2 CAPTURE 
 
Hutha Sarma, and Steven Ogunwumi 
Corning Incorporated 
Corning, NY, USA 
 
ABSTRACT 
Porous ceramic honeycomb structures are being explored as purely structural members to 
support catalysts used in various filtration applications like CO2 capture. High temperature firing, 
an essential part of conventional ceramic fabrication, is highly complex and capital intensive, 
besides having a large CO2 footprint. Also, unlike the underlying ceramic, most of the catalysts 
cannot withstand high temperatures. Hence, the manufacture of these articles is a two–step 
process: (1) Production of the ceramic substrate; (2) Subsequent application of the catalyst.  
In this paper, a novel low temperature firing/heat treatment approach for ceramic 
processing is proposed, so as to render high temperature firing unnecessary.  Preliminary data 
indicating that this low temperature approach could yield extruded ware with sufficient strength 
so as to be attractive to applications where refractoriness is not a premium, is also presented. 
Such a low temperature processing would render co-extrusion of catalysts and sorbents (e.g., 
zeolites) feasible. This in turn would not only lead to a simple, one–step process, but also result 
in a “greener” process with low CO2 footprint, thus making it a sustainable and attractive 
approach for filtration and sequestration of CO2 applications. 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Typical ceramic honeycomb manufacturing process is very complex, and involves 
batching, forming (extrusion), drying, firing, and finishing. Of these, firing is by far the most 
capital intensive and time consuming.  It is sensitive to raw materials, product attributes 
(porosity, coefficient of thermal expansion [CTE], etc.). However, high temperature (>1000 C) 
is not mandatory for most catalytic and separation or filtration applications. Since costs & 
difficulty to fire do not scale linearly with firing temperature, high temperature firing is to be 
avoided if possible, especially for applications that do not need refractoriness. Some of the 
reasons for firing ceramics are as follows: 
To burn out forming/processing (e.g., extrusion) enablers (e.g., Hydroxy Methyl Cellulose, 
Sodium stearate, etc.), and pore formers (e.g., starch, graphite, etc.): This can be achieved at 
temperatures below 800 – 1000 C. 
To form the desired phases, in–situ (reaction sintering): This is not necessary, if the final 
phases are already present in the batch materials.  
To impart strength (sintering) 
To expose the ware to a temperature greater than operating temperature, so as to avoid 
shrinkage during operation: This is not necessary if the body has negligible shrinkage. 
 
It is evident that any replacement to firing, should meet the last two aforementioned 
objectives: (1) Ware has sufficient strength; and (2) It meets shrinkage requirements. Here an 
alternative, low temperature firing approach to honeycomb fabrication is proposed, and some 
preliminary data regarding strength and shrinkage is  presented. Throughout this paper, the 
phrase low temperature firing is used to refer to firing temperatures <1000 C, with the typical 
firing temperature used being between 600 – 800 C.   
 
 
 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
166
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Novel Low Temperature Ceramics for CO2 Capture
METHODOLOGY 
The proposed approach, involves no high temperature firing for fabrication of ceramic 
bodies. It is fairly generic, and could be used whether the ceramic ware is to be made of silica, 
SiC, mullite or ash. The chief ingredients (see Fig. 1) of the novel, low-temperature-fired 
compositions that have been included in this study, are as follows: 
 
CERAMIC FILLER 
Any ceramic particles may be used. In-fact, due to its cost effectiveness, as well as 
environmental impact, ash from power plants is a very attractive candidate. Preliminary results 
validate ash to be a viable candidate. Since the filler is the dominant phase in the composition, 
ranging between 40 – 90 wt.%, a low CTE material would lead to a better thermal shock 
resistance (TSR) for the extruded ware, if the application requires considerable thermal 
gradients. To ameliorate thermal shock, especially when using materials with high intrinsic CTE, 
segmentation may also be considered.  
 
FIBER PHASE 
Fibers are used to enhance the strength and toughness of extruded ware2. Refractory 
ceramic fibers (RCFs, e.g., asbestos, rock wool, glass fibers), though excellent tougheners, are 
being phased out, and hence, have become costly. Any material with aspect ratio would behave 
akin to fibers. Wollastonite and halloysite are examples of cheap, benign, natural materials. In 
the compositions tested, the fiber phase ranged from 5 – 50 wt.%. 
 
HIGH TEMPERATURE BINDER 
Any colloidal phase may be used to bond the filler and fiber particles, thus imparting 
strength to the extruded body at high temperatures (>300 C), where no organic binder is likely to 
survive. Due to its extensive usage as binder, colloidal silica would be an excellent choice for 
this phase. The amount of binder used could be between 5 – 25 wt.%. 
 
ORGANIC ADDITIVES 
Their chief purpose is to provide batch rheology that would enable extrusion and impart 
some green strength to the extruded ware. The amount of such extrusion aids is purely dictated 
by processing constraints, and since these materials are burnt away during firing, and hence 
absent from the final article, the actual amounts used are of no practical relevance. Hydroxy 
Methyl Cellulose, Poly Vinyl Alcohol, and Sodium Stearate are a few examples of extrusion aids 
extensively used in the industry.  
 
MISCELLANEOUS ADDITIVES 
Dispersants, humectants, plasticizers or non-aqueous mixing media may be added if 
needed.  
 
Batches consisting of the aforementioned ingredients were prepared. After thorough mixing 
of the dry ingredients, liquids were added so as to make an extrudable paste. These pastes were 
subsequently extruded in to rods and honeycombs, dried, and subsequently fired at various 
temperatures. Physical property data like shrinkage, porosity and modulus of rupture (MOR) 
were obtained for these various samples. 
 
RESULTS & DISCUSSION 
In the subsequent sections, properties of a few compositions are presented in order to 
make a case for a non-fired  substrate approach as an alternative to firing. Fused silica and 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
·
167
Novel Low Temperature Ceramics for CO2 Capture
Wollastonite were chosen as the ceramic filler and fiber phases respectively. Detailed 
experimentation regarding optimization of the various batch contents were carried out, but for 
the sake of brevity and proprietary reasons, are not presented here.  
Images of extruded ware are shown in Fig. 2. MOR is frequently used as a proxy for 
strength. MOR and CTE for cast sheets (and fired at 600 C for 3hrs.) as a function of their 
Wollastonite content are shown in Fig. 3 and Fig. 4 respectively. It is evident from these plots 
that Wollastonite levels beyond 25 wt.% only increase the CTE at no significant MOR 
improvements. Wollastonite level could be adjusted to yield ware with the required MOR and 
CTE.  Similar data may be obtained for any other fiber phases as well.  
 
STRENGTH OF LOW TEMPERATURE FIRED WARE 
MOR values of extruded rods, for some compositions, as a function of calcination (firing) 
temperature are shown in Fig. 5. As is evident from these data, MOR increases with firing 
temperature, and several compositions have strengths comparable to or better than fired 
cordierite bodies, of comparable porosity, after firing at just 600 C, which is well below the 
usual cordierite firing temperature (~1425 C). 
 
SHRINKAGE 
Shrinkage and porosity data for one of the compositions tested,  are presented in Fig. 6. 
The substrate is porous for coatings. Extruded ware from this composition shrinks by 0.5% 
between 600 and 1000 C, a value small enough that it may be accounted for via other 
engineering solutions (e.g., usage of compressible materials as fixture supports2). Thus, ware 
fired at 600 C may be used up to an operating temperature of ~1000 C. 
 
CO2 CAPTURE TEST  
Testing was performed using a molecular sieve zeolite that has an adsorption capacity of 
2.8 mol CO2/Kg sorbent in powder form. Good adhesion between the extruded honeycomb and 
coating was observed (for e.g., see Fig. 2). A capacity of 2.6 mol CO2/Kg was measured when 
the sorbent was applied on to a honeycomb and tested. The test condition was 90% CO2/10% N2 
at 500 SCCM. The equilibrium adsorption capacity of the powdered sorbent measured showed a 
minor change in the results obtained, compared to the un-fired or low temperature honeycomb 
comprising the same sorbent. Further process optimization is expected to make the change 
negligible. 
 
SUMMARY 
High temperature firing (firing temperature >1000 C), an essential part of conventional 
ceramic fabrication, is highly complex and capital intensive. Preliminary data indicates that a 
novel low temperature approach, that does not require high temperature firing, could yield 
extruded ware with sufficient strength so as to be attractive to applications where refractoriness 
is not a premium. Such a low temperature processing would render co-extrusion of catalysts and 
sorbents (e.g., zeolites) feasible. Preliminary results of the concept using a molecular sieve 
adsorbent was very promising. The equilibrium adsorption capacity of the powdered sorbent 
measured showed a minor change in the results obtained compared to the low temperature fired 
honeycomb comprising the sorbent. Further process optimization is expected to make the change 
negligible.

EFFECTS OF SiC PARTICLE SIZE AND SINTERING TEMPERATURE ON 
MICROSTRUCTURE OF POROUS SiC CERAMICS BASED ON IN–SITU GRAIN 
GROWTH 
Katsumi Yoshida, Chin-Chet See, Satoshi Yokoyama, Toyohiko Yano 
Research Laboratory for Nuclear Reactors, Tokyo Institute of Technology 
Tokyo, JAPAN 
 
ABSTRACT 
Porous silicon carbide (SiC) ceramics were fabricated based on in-situ grain growth, and the 
effects of -SiC particle size and sintering temperature on the microstructure of the porous SiC 
ceramics were investigated. In the case of submicron-sized (average particle size; 0.3 m) -SiC 
particles, grain growth was promoted and the formation of plate-like grains started at 2050oC, 
and the porous SiC ceramics consisted of plate-like SiC grains of around 10-20 m at 2150oC. 
Whereas SiC grains were not grown in porous SiC ceramics using micron-sized (2.3 m) -SiC 
particles sintered at 2000oC, the porous SiC ceramics with a unique morphology of entangled 
large plate-like SiC grains of about 30-40 m in maximum were achieved at 2150oC. XRD 
analysis suggested that the formation of 6H phase was promoted in the porous SiC ceramics 
using the submicron-sized or the micron-sized -SiC powder with the small amount of Al and B 
additions at 2150oC, and the plate-like SiC grains mainly corresponded to 6H phase. On the other 
hand, coarse -SiC particles (5.9 m) did not grow into plate-like shapes at 2150oC in spite of 
Al-B-C addition. 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Silicon carbide (SiC) is one of the attractive materials for structural applications such as 
aerospace industries, nuclear applications and high-temperature gas turbine since it shows high 
thermal and chemical stability, high stiffness, high hardness, high thermal conductivity, excellent 
oxidation, corrosion and wear resistance, high mechanical strength at high-temperatures, low 
thermal expansion, and good resistance to high-energy neutron irradiation.1-7 Recently, porous 
ceramics have received great attention in many fields. Porous ceramics have been traditionally 
used as heat insulating materials and refractories. Furthermore, porous ceramics have been 
expected to be applied as catalyst carrier, diesel particulate filter (DPF), membranes for gas 
separation, filters for water purification, adsorbent, electrodes for batteries, and bone-graft 
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII.
Edited by Roger Narayan and Paolo Colombo
 
Copyright © 2015 The American Ceramic Society.
174
·
Advances in Bioceramics and Porous Ceramics VII
Effects of SiC Particle Size and Sintering Temperature on Microstructure
substitutes and so on.8,9 Currently, porous SiC ceramics has been commercially used for DPF.9
Pore size control and surface functionalization of porous ceramics have been strongly 
required to extend their applications. Present authors have proposed the surface functionalization 
of porous ceramics based on in-situ grain growth.10,11 Figure 1 shows the concept of the porous 
ceramics with in-situ grain growth. The authors paid attention to the interesting structures seen in 
nature such as calcareous caves for the surface functionalization of porous ceramics. In our study, 
in-situ grain growth has been defined as the grain growth and the grain orientation on the 
sintered surface such as the surface and/or inside of pore walls and frameworks in porous 
ceramics, and we have tried to fabricate porous ceramics based on in-situ grain growth. In our 
previous study, porous SiC ceramics with in-situ plate-like grain growth were successfully 
achieved by pressureless-sintering at 2150oC for 30 min using submicron-sized -SiC powder 
and 0.1 wt% aluminum (Al) - 0.15 wt% boron (B) - 1.00 wt% carbon (C) additives.10,11 In 
addition, aluminum nitrate enneahydrate (Al(NO3)3•9H2O) was effective to enhance plate-like 
grain growth in porous SiC ceramics because Al from Al(NO3)3•9H2O would be distributed 
homogeneously on the SiC powder rather than metallic Al powder. The pore size distribution of 
the porous SiC ceramics with in-situ grain growth was bimodal, and larger pores (around 8 m) 
and smaller pores (around 1 
m) would be formed by pore formers and the gaps among 
plate-like grains, respectively.
In this study, three kinds of -SiC powders with different particle sizes were used as the 
starting materials, and the effects of 
-SiC particle size and sintering temperature on 
microstructure of porous SiC ceramics based on in-situ grain growth were investigated. 
 
Figure 1. The concept of the porous ceramics with in-situ grain growth.
